Energy and Utilities English

An energy and utilities English curriculum for reliability, outages, grid operations, safety, regulatory filings, renewables integration, infrastructure projects, customer communication, and risk reporting.

For utility operations staff, energy project managers, grid planners, field supervisors, regulatory affairs teams, renewable-energy developers, customer operations teams, and infrastructure leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Give a useful outage message while distinguishing affected scope, assessment status, and the next update.
  • Escalate conflicting equipment references and use exact repeat-back without creating an operational instruction.
  • Explain a rate proposal and calculate a conditional bill example without implying approval.
  • Explain renewable-project review status without confusing capacity, application progress, and operating permission.
  • Compare a stated cost basis, test an assumption, and keep the purchase decision separate from partial arithmetic.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Only qualified personnel using applicable authorized procedures may make operational decisions. No dialogue authorizes switching, access, energization, or restoration.

01Grid Reliability and Outage ResponseAn update time is not a restoration promise+

Your goal

Give a useful outage message while distinguishing affected scope, assessment status, and the next update.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

An update time is not a restoration promise

At 16:10 local time, a verified operations update lists 420 affected service points in the Oak district. The cause and estimated restoration time remain under assessment. A crew is assigned, but its arrival has not been confirmed. A draft public message promises restoration by 18:00, although that figure is an unverified internal planning assumption. Communications officer Nia and operations liaison Paul must correct the draft. Paul will provide the next verified status at 16:40 local time, even if no restoration estimate is available. The affected count is a snapshot, not a prediction of the final scope.

Nia: Communications officer / Paul: Operations liaison

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is verified at 16:10?

Answer and explanation

C. 420 affected service points in the Oak district

The supplied operations update verifies the affected count and area, not cause or restoration time.

2. What does 16:40 represent?

Answer and explanation

A. The next status update

Paul commits to another verified status update, even if restoration timing remains unavailable.

3. Which crew statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. A crew is assigned; arrival is unconfirmed.

Assignment is confirmed, but the brief explicitly leaves crew arrival unconfirmed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A service-point count is not necessarily a count of individual people. The 420 figure describes the verified snapshot at 16:10. Do not turn it into a final impact total or silently change the population being counted.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
electric grid
The interconnected infrastructure delivering electricity from sources to users.maintain the electric grid
transmission
Bulk movement of electricity over the relevant high-voltage network.coordinate transmission operations
distribution
Delivery of electricity through the network serving end users.restore distribution service
feeder
A circuit supplying a section of the distribution network.identify the affected feeder
substation
A facility connecting, transforming, or controlling parts of an electrical network.identify the supplying substation
service point
A defined point at which service is supplied or measured.count affected service points
outage
A loss or unavailability of electricity service or equipment function.report an outage
outage footprint
The area or set of service points affected by an outage.verify the outage footprint
estimated restoration time (ERT)
The current estimate of when interrupted service may be restored.update the estimated restoration time
damage assessment
Evaluation of damage and relevant conditions following an event.coordinate damage assessment
crew assignment
Allocation of a crew to an identified task or response.confirm the crew assignment
crew arrival
The crew reaching the relevant location, distinct from being assigned.verify crew arrival
dispatch
Coordination and direction of resources or operating activity.contact dispatch
outage management system (OMS)
A system supporting outage tracking, analysis, and response coordination.update the outage management system
supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA)
Systems used to monitor and control relevant industrial processes.interpret SCADA information
telemetry
Measurements or status information transmitted from remote equipment.verify telemetry status
load
Electrical demand placed on the system or equipment.monitor system load
peak demand
The highest demand over a specified period and measurement basis.assess peak demand
kilowatt (kW)
A unit of power equal to one thousand watts.state demand in kilowatts
kilowatt-hour (kWh)
A unit of energy equal to one kilowatt sustained for one hour.measure energy in kilowatt-hours
restoration sequence
The authorized order of activities or service recovery during restoration.communicate the restoration sequence
status snapshot
A report of conditions as known at a stated time.timestamp the status snapshot
planning assumption
An unverified condition or estimate used in developing a plan.label a planning assumption
update commitment
A promise to provide new status information at a specified point.keep the update commitment

The 16:40 commitment concerns information. It is not a crew-arrival or restoration time. Also, a customer outage report does not establish that equipment is electrically safe; operational status requires authorized verification.

C Notice the language

Uncertainty needs a useful next step

Customers can hear we do not know as abandonment if the message stops there. Give the confirmed scope, explain what remains under assessment, and state the next update commitment. Do not manufacture confidence by converting a planning assumption into a promise.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Timestamp the message
This is the verified status as of 16:10 local time.
Name the scope
The update lists 420 affected service points in the Oak district.
Limit the count
That is the current snapshot, not a final impact total.
State the assessment
The cause remains under assessment.
Separate the estimate
A restoration estimate is not yet available.
Correct unsupported certainty
We cannot publish 18:00 as a confirmed restoration time.
Explain the source
That figure was an unverified planning assumption.
Describe crew status
A crew is assigned; arrival is not yet confirmed.
Make a clear commitment
Our next status update will be at 16:40 local time.
Preserve the distinction
The update time is not a promise of restored service.
Acknowledge disruption
I understand that the uncertainty makes planning difficult.
Avoid a guessed cause
We will not name a cause before it is verified.
Coordinate the channels
Correct the website and customer-service message together.
Keep the clock basis
Use local time consistently in the public update.
Maintain contact
We will update you even if the estimate remains unavailable.
Close accurately
Share the confirmed facts and the questions still under assessment.
6 language and precision notes
As of
Connects a count or status to its timestamp.
Affected service points
Names the measured unit without equating it to individual people.
Assigned versus arrived
Separates allocation from physical arrival.
Not yet available
Preserves uncertainty without implying that no work is happening.
By 18:00
Creates a latest-time promise that is unsupported here.
Next update
Identifies a communication commitment, not an operational outcome.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which public update matches the record?

Answer and explanation

C. At 16:10, 420 service points are affected; restoration timing is under assessment; next update 16:40.

The accurate update preserves the count, timestamp, unresolved estimate, and communication commitment.

2. Which phrase wrongly strengthens the crew status?

Answer and explanation

A. Crews are on site.

On site claims arrival, which the supplied facts do not confirm.

3. What should happen if no estimate exists at 16:40?

Answer and explanation

D. Provide the promised update and state that timing remains under assessment.

The commitment is to provide status information, not to produce a certainty that does not exist.

4. What does the 420 figure count?

Answer and explanation

A. Service points in the verified snapshot

The supplied unit is affected service points, and the figure is limited to a timestamped snapshot.

E Conversations

An update time is not a restoration promise

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Communications officer / Paul: Operations liaison

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • outage footprint
  • crew arrival
  • crew assignment
  • Damage assessment
  • outage management system
  • update commitment
  • estimated restoration time
  • planning assumption
  • restoration sequence
  • status snapshot
  1. Nia
    The draft promises restoration by eighteen hundred. Before publishing, can you confirm that time's source and whether it is verified?
  2. Paul
    It is a 1, not a confirmed estimate. Remove the promise. As of sixteen ten local time, operations still has the cause and restoration timing under assessment.
  3. Nia
    What can I say confidently? The draft also mentions the Oak district and four hundred twenty affected service points, but I want the exact scope.
  4. Paul
    Those are the verified figures in the 2. Keep the timestamp attached. The number may change as information improves, and it is not a count of individual people.
  5. Nia
    I will retain service points rather than replace that with residents. Customers also want to know whether anybody is working on the problem right now.
  6. Paul
    We can confirm the 3. Arrival has not been verified, so do not say crews are on site. Assignment and physical arrival are different stages in the response.
  7. Nia
    Customer service is already saying on site. I will ask them to change that to assigned, with arrival unconfirmed.
  8. Paul
    Exactly. The 4 supports tracking, but we still need to communicate the actual verified status. A populated field does not justify a stronger statement than its source.
  9. Nia
    Can we at least promise another update? Saying the estimate is unavailable without a next contact point sounds as though we have nothing further to offer.
  10. Paul
    Yes. Make an 5 for sixteen forty local time. I will provide the verified position then, even if the restoration estimate remains unavailable and we need to say so.
  11. Nia
    I will put the update time on its own line and label it clearly. It must not look like a deadline for the lights to come back on.
  12. Paul
    Keep it separate from 6. Customers may scan only the number. The label must make clear that sixteen forty is our next communication, not an expected restoration or crew arrival.
  13. Nia
    The draft also guesses that a failed transformer caused the outage. Our record does not verify that cause.
  14. Paul
    Remove that diagnosis. 7 and other relevant checks remain with qualified operations personnel. We should not turn a plausible equipment explanation into a finding because it sounds more informative.
  15. Nia
    I will correct the website and customer-service script together. Leaving the eighteen hundred promise in either channel would continue the confusion.
  16. Paul
    Agreed. Describe the 8 consistently as well. We have a verified Oak district snapshot, not evidence that every nearby district or every person in that area is affected.
  17. Nia
    The draft also says the affected equipment is safe because service is off. I will remove that; the outage record does not establish safety.
  18. Paul
    Preserve that boundary without improvising instructions. The 9 and equipment safety remain governed by authorized procedures. This message reports customer-service status; it does not authorize anyone to approach or operate equipment.
  19. Nia
    The revised message will give the timestamp, affected service points, unresolved cause and timing, assigned crew with arrival unconfirmed, and the sixteen forty update.
  20. Paul
    That is accurate. If 10 or restoration information is confirmed before the next update, we can communicate the verified change. Until then, keep the difference between known status and expectation visible.

Answers and explanations
  1. planning assumption Turn 2. A planning assumption is an unverified basis for a plan, not a confirmed restoration estimate suitable for a promise.
  2. status snapshot Turn 4. The status snapshot describes known conditions at a stated time, not a final total or prediction of the outage's full scope.
  3. crew assignment Turn 6. Crew assignment means a crew has been allocated; it does not establish arrival, repair progress, or restoration.
  4. outage management system Turn 8. The outage management system supports tracking the incident, but communications must preserve the actual verified status recorded through operations.
  5. update commitment Turn 10. An update commitment promises further information at a specified time, not restoration or a completed assessment by that time.
  6. estimated restoration time Turn 12. Estimated restoration time concerns when service may return; the next communication time is not a substitute for that operational estimate.
  7. Damage assessment Turn 14. Damage assessment evaluates actual damage and conditions; an unverified guess about equipment failure must not replace its findings.
  8. outage footprint Turn 16. The outage footprint is the verified affected area or service-point set; here it is the Oak district snapshot, not an invented wider impact.
  9. restoration sequence Turn 18. The restoration sequence is an authorized operational matter; a public communications update does not direct switching or establish electrical safety.
  10. crew arrival Turn 20. Crew arrival is a distinct verified event that can be reported when confirmed, without automatically implying repair completion or restoration.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nia: The draft promises restoration by eighteen hundred. Before publishing, can you confirm that time's source and whether it is verified?
  2. Paul: It is a planning assumption, not a confirmed estimate. Remove the promise. As of sixteen ten local time, operations still has the cause and restoration timing under assessment.
  3. Nia: What can I say confidently? The draft also mentions the Oak district and four hundred twenty affected service points, but I want the exact scope.
  4. Paul: Those are the verified figures in the status snapshot. Keep the timestamp attached. The number may change as information improves, and it is not a count of individual people.
  5. Nia: I will retain service points rather than replace that with residents. Customers also want to know whether anybody is working on the problem right now.
  6. Paul: We can confirm the crew assignment. Arrival has not been verified, so do not say crews are on site. Assignment and physical arrival are different stages in the response.
  7. Nia: Customer service is already saying on site. I will ask them to change that to assigned, with arrival unconfirmed.
  8. Paul: Exactly. The outage management system supports tracking, but we still need to communicate the actual verified status. A populated field does not justify a stronger statement than its source.
  9. Nia: Can we at least promise another update? Saying the estimate is unavailable without a next contact point sounds as though we have nothing further to offer.
  10. Paul: Yes. Make an update commitment for sixteen forty local time. I will provide the verified position then, even if the restoration estimate remains unavailable and we need to say so.
  11. Nia: I will put the update time on its own line and label it clearly. It must not look like a deadline for the lights to come back on.
  12. Paul: Keep it separate from estimated restoration time. Customers may scan only the number. The label must make clear that sixteen forty is our next communication, not an expected restoration or crew arrival.
  13. Nia: The draft also guesses that a failed transformer caused the outage. Our record does not verify that cause.
  14. Paul: Remove that diagnosis. Damage assessment and other relevant checks remain with qualified operations personnel. We should not turn a plausible equipment explanation into a finding because it sounds more informative.
  15. Nia: I will correct the website and customer-service script together. Leaving the eighteen hundred promise in either channel would continue the confusion.
  16. Paul: Agreed. Describe the outage footprint consistently as well. We have a verified Oak district snapshot, not evidence that every nearby district or every person in that area is affected.
  17. Nia: The draft also says the affected equipment is safe because service is off. I will remove that; the outage record does not establish safety.
  18. Paul: Preserve that boundary without improvising instructions. The restoration sequence and equipment safety remain governed by authorized procedures. This message reports customer-service status; it does not authorize anyone to approach or operate equipment.
  19. Nia: The revised message will give the timestamp, affected service points, unresolved cause and timing, assigned crew with arrival unconfirmed, and the sixteen forty update.
  20. Paul: That is accurate. If crew arrival or restoration information is confirmed before the next update, we can communicate the verified change. Until then, keep the difference between known status and expectation visible.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Restoration time remains under assessment

Fictional outage-communications exercise: an affected area is identified, but the restoration estimate is still being assessed. No operating instructions are given.

  1. Outage communications officer: The affected area is identified, but the draft gives a firm restoration time we haven't verified.
  2. Customer operations manager: I wanted customers to have something definite. What can we actually confirm right now?
  3. Outage communications officer: We can name the reported area and say restoration timing remains under assessment.
  4. Customer operations manager: Does identifying the feeder mean the cause and repair plan are also established?
  5. Outage communications officer: No. Those are separate questions, and this update doesn't supply verified answers to them.
  6. Customer operations manager: Then I'll remove the firm time and explain that the assessment is continuing.
  7. Outage communications officer: Please keep critical-customer coordination within the approved process rather than making individual restoration promises.
  8. Customer operations manager: Understood. Who will request the next verified status from the responsible operations team?
  9. Outage communications officer: I'll make that request and distinguish any confirmed change from estimates still under review.
  10. Customer operations manager: I'll issue the corrected status without turning an unknown restoration time into a guarantee.
Some streets restored, others still affected

Separate fictional case: service restoration is confirmed for Oak Street, while adjacent Pine Street remains affected; no cause or completion time is supplied.

  1. Community liaison: Oak Street is restored. Can our update say the neighborhood's outage has ended?
  2. Outage coordinator: Not the whole neighborhood. Pine Street remains affected in the current verified status.
  3. Community liaison: I see. The completed restoration applies to Oak Street, not every nearby address.
  4. Outage coordinator: Exactly. Geographic precision matters when customers use the message to plan their next steps.
  5. Community liaison: Should I infer that Pine Street will follow soon because the streets are adjacent?
  6. Outage coordinator: No. Proximity doesn't establish restoration timing, and we don't have a verified estimate here.
  7. Community liaison: I'll name both streets and separate restored service from the area still affected.
  8. Outage coordinator: Good. Also keep any feeder explanation limited to information confirmed by the operations team.
  9. Community liaison: I'll request another status check rather than describe the remaining outage as nearly finished.
  10. Outage coordinator: That gives a useful progress update while preserving the uncertainty about Pine Street's restoration.
Reports received are not repairs completed

Separate fictional service-desk case: twenty outage reports have been logged and grouped for review; field assessment remains pending.

  1. Service desk supervisor: We've logged twenty outage reports. The dashboard says twenty cases handled this morning.
  2. Reliability analyst: Handled is ambiguous. Does it mean logged, assessed, or resolved in this report?
  3. Service desk supervisor: Only logged and grouped. The field assessment is still pending with the responsible team.
  4. Reliability analyst: Then use those completed actions explicitly. Otherwise readers may infer twenty completed restorations.
  5. Service desk supervisor: Could we call them twenty separate outages since each customer submitted a report?
  6. Reliability analyst: Not from that count alone. Several reports may concern the same event; grouping needs verification.
  7. Service desk supervisor: I'll say twenty reports received and grouped, with assessment and restoration status still pending.
  8. Reliability analyst: Good. Keep report volume separate from the reliability measures used for verified outage events.
  9. Service desk supervisor: I'll ask the operations liaison for the assessment update and avoid announcing a completion time.
  10. Reliability analyst: That makes the dashboard more informative without claiming work that the records don't establish.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An update time is not a restoration promise
  1. Read the corrected dialogue, keeping 420 service points distinct from a count of people.
  2. Switch roles; emphasize assigned versus arrived and 16:40 as an information update, not restoration.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Correct a second outage message

At 09:20 local time, 180 service points are affected in Pine district. Restoration timing is under assessment. The next information update is 09:50.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Officer: "The verified affected count is ___ service points."

Answer and explanation

C. 180

One hundred eighty is the supplied service-point count for this snapshot.

2. Liaison: "The affected district is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Pine

Pine is the named district in the separate outage case.

3. Officer: "The next information update is at ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 09:50

Nine fifty is the stated communication time, not a restoration estimate.

4. Liaison: "Restoration timing remains under ___."

Answer and explanation

D. assessment

The brief explicitly leaves restoration timing under assessment rather than confirmed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Grid Reliability and Outage Response
Original fictional case: At 16:10 local time, a verified operations update lists 420 affected service points in the Oak district. The cause and estimated restoration time remain under assessment. A crew is assigned, but its arrival has not been confirmed. A draft public message promises restoration by 18:00, although that figure is an unverified internal planning assumption. Communications officer Nia and operations liaison Paul must correct the draft. Paul will provide the next verified status at 16:40 local time, even if no restoration estimate is available. The affected count is a snapshot, not a prediction of the final scope.
Language workshop: An update time is not a restoration promise
Communication goal: Give a useful outage message while distinguishing affected scope, assessment status, and the next update.
Language guidance: A service-point count is not necessarily a count of individual people. The 420 figure describes the verified snapshot at 16:10. Do not turn it into a final impact total or silently change the population being counted.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is the verified status as of 16:10 local time. / The update lists 420 affected service points in the Oak district. / That is the current snapshot, not a final impact total. / The cause remains under assessment. / A restoration estimate is not yet available. / We cannot publish 18:00 as a confirmed restoration time. / That figure was an unverified planning assumption. / A crew is assigned; arrival is not yet confirmed. / Our next status update will be at 16:40 local time. / The update time is not a promise of restored service. / I understand that the uncertainty makes planning difficult. / We will not name a cause before it is verified. / Correct the website and customer-service message together. / Use local time consistently in the public update. / We will update you even if the estimate remains unavailable. / Share the confirmed facts and the questions still under assessment.

/ outage footprint: The area or set of service points affected by an outage. / estimated restoration time (ERT): The current estimate of when interrupted service may be restored. / damage assessment: Evaluation of damage and relevant conditions following an event. / crew assignment: Allocation of a crew to an identified task or response. / crew arrival: The crew reaching the relevant location, distinct from being assigned. / dispatch: Coordination and direction of resources or operating activity. / outage management system (OMS): A system supporting outage tracking, analysis, and response Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

REFERENCE
Course: Energy and Utilities English
Professional audience: utility operations staff, energy project managers, grid planners, field supervisors, regulatory affairs teams, renewable-energy developers, customer operations teams, and infrastructure leaders
Scope: All organizations, projects, figures, equipment identifiers, and local processes are fictional. This is English-language practice, not electrical, emergency, financial, regulatory, or engineering instruction. Only qualified personnel using applicable authorized procedures may make operational decisions. No dialogue authorizes switching, access, energization, or restoration.
Lesson 01: Grid Reliability and Outage Response
Original fictional case: At 16:10 local time, a verified operations update lists 420 affected service points in the Oak district. The cause and estimated restoration time remain under assessment. A crew is assigned, but its arrival has not been confirmed. A draft public message promises restoration by 18:00, although that figure is an unverified internal planning assumption. Communications officer Nia and operations liaison Paul must correct the draft. Paul will provide the next verified status at 16:40 local time, even if no restoration estimate is available. The affected count is a snapshot, not a prediction of the final scope.
Language workshop: An update time is not a restoration promise
Communication goal: Give a useful outage message while distinguishing affected scope, assessment status, and the next update.
Language guidance: A service-point count is not necessarily a count of individual people. The 420 figure describes the verified snapshot at 16:10. Do not turn it into a final impact total or silently change the population being counted.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is the verified status as of 16:10 local time. / The update lists 420 affected service points in the Oak district. / That is the current snapshot, not a final impact total. / The cause remains under assessment. / A restoration estimate is not yet available. / We cannot publish 18:00 as a confirmed restoration time. / That figure was an unverified planning assumption. / A crew is assigned; arrival is not yet confirmed. / Our next status update will be at 16:40 local time. / The update time is not a promise of restored service. / I understand that the uncertainty makes planning difficult. / We will not name a cause before it is verified. / Correct the website and customer-service message together. / Use local time consistently in the public update. / We will update you even if the estimate remains unavailable. / Share the confirmed facts and the questions still under assessment.
Vocabulary: electric grid: The interconnected infrastructure delivering electricity from sources to users. / transmission: Bulk movement of electricity over the relevant high-voltage network. / distribution: Delivery of electricity through the network serving end users. / feeder: A circuit supplying a section of the distribution network. / substation: A facility connecting, transforming, or controlling parts of an electrical network. / service point: A defined point at which service is supplied or measured. / outage: A loss or unavailability of electricity service or equipment function. / outage footprint: The area or set of service points affected by an outage. / estimated restoration time (ERT): The current estimate of when interrupted service may be restored. / damage assessment: Evaluation of damage and relevant conditions following an event. / crew assignment: Allocation of a crew to an identified task or response. / crew arrival: The crew reaching the relevant location, distinct from being assigned. / dispatch: Coordination and direction of resources or operating activity. / outage management system (OMS): A system supporting outage tracking, analysis, and response coordination. / supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA): Systems used to monitor and control relevant industrial processes. / telemetry: Measurements or status information transmitted from remote equipment. / load: Electrical demand placed on the system or equipment. / peak demand: The highest demand over a specified period and measurement basis. / kilowatt (kW): A unit of power equal to one thousand watts. / kilowatt-hour (kWh): A unit of energy equal to one kilowatt sustained for one hour. / restoration sequence: The authorized order of activities or service recovery during restoration. / status snapshot: A report of conditions as known at a stated time. / planning assumption: An unverified condition or estimate used in developing a plan. / update commitment: A promise to provide new status information at a specified point.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue, keeping 420 service points distinct from a count of people. Switch roles; emphasize assigned versus arrived and 16:40 as an information update, not restoration. Read the checked Pine exchange twice with 180 service points and a 09:50 update, without adding a restoration promise.
Partner: Nia: Communications officer / Paul: Operations liaison
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 09:20 local time, 180 service points are affected in Pine district. Restoration timing is under assessment. The next information update is 09:50.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nia: The draft also says the affected equipment is safe because service is off. I will remove that; the outage record does not establish safety.
Paul: Preserve that boundary without improvising instructions. The restoration sequence and equipment safety remain governed by authorized procedures. This message reports customer-service status; it does not authorize anyone to approach or operate equipment.
Nia: The revised message will give the timestamp, affected service points, unresolved cause and timing, assigned crew with arrival unconfirmed, and the sixteen forty update.
Paul: That is accurate. If crew arrival or restoration information is confirmed before the next update, we can communicate the verified change. Until then, keep the difference between known status and expectation visible.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

02Safety, Field Work, and SwitchingTwo identifiers must not be treated as interchangeable+

Your goal

Escalate conflicting equipment references and use exact repeat-back without creating an operational instruction.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Two identifiers must not be treated as interchangeable

A fictional field work package identifies equipment Q27, while its associated switching request identifies Q72. The team has not established which identifier is correct. Field lead Luis has placed the affected task on hold under the local process; no operational action is authorized from these conflicting documents. System operator Farah will coordinate reconciliation through the authorized document and operating procedures. Neither identifier is to be silently corrected, and neither the equipment state nor a safe work condition can be inferred from this dialogue. The language task is to communicate the discrepancy, hold, owner, and next review accurately.

Luis: Field lead / Farah: System operator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which identifiers conflict?

Answer and explanation

B. Q27 and Q72

The work package identifies Q27 while the switching request identifies Q72.

2. Which identifier is correct from the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

C. It has not been established.

The case provides no verified basis for selecting either conflicting identifier.

3. What does a repeat-back establish?

Answer and explanation

D. That the discrepancy and current hold were understood

Repeating the message confirms communication, not the equipment condition or operating authority.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Q27 and Q72 contain the same digits in a different order. That resemblance does not prove a harmless typo or identify the correct asset. Keep both references intact in the discrepancy report until authorized reconciliation is complete.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
equipment identifier
The exact reference used to identify a particular item of equipment.verify the equipment identifier
asset tag
A label or code assigned to an asset for identification.reconcile the asset tag
work package
The documents and information governing a defined work task.review the work package
switching request
A request for specified switching activity through the authorized process.check the switching request
switching order
An authorized sequence or instruction for switching under the applicable process.verify the switching order
single-line diagram
A simplified diagram representing electrical connections with single lines.consult the current single-line diagram
system operator
An authorized person responsible for specified system-operating functions.contact the system operator
field lead
The person leading the relevant field team or task.notify the field lead
energized
Connected to an electrical energy source or otherwise carrying electrical potential as applicable.verify energized status
deenergized
Without connection to an electrical potential source, stored charge, or voltage relative to earth; not synonymous with safe to work.establish deenergized status
isolation
Separation from relevant energy sources under the applicable authorized process.verify isolation requirements
backfeed
Electrical energy supplied from an unexpected or alternative direction or source.assess backfeed hazards
grounding
Establishing the required electrical connection to ground under applicable procedures.follow authorized grounding requirements
induced voltage
Voltage produced by electromagnetic influence from another source.assess induced-voltage hazards
arc flash
A hazardous release of energy associated with an electrical arc.assess arc-flash hazards
minimum approach distance
A required separation from electrical hazards under applicable conditions and rules.observe the applicable minimum approach distance
qualified worker
A worker with the required training and competence for the specified task.verify qualified-worker status
operating authority
The assigned authority to make or direct specified operational decisions.preserve operating authority
clearance
A formal authorization or controlled status with a defined meaning under the operating procedure.verify the clearance conditions
lockout/tagout
Procedures using locks and tags within the applicable hazardous-energy control process.follow applicable lockout/tagout procedures
read-back
Repetition of a received message to check its accuracy.perform an exact read-back
hold point
A boundary beyond which the affected activity must not proceed until requirements are met.maintain the hold point
document reconciliation
Resolving conflicting information through the authorized review and record process.complete document reconciliation
revision control
Management of document versions and their applicable status.maintain revision control

Read-back confirms what was communicated. It does not establish equipment identity, isolation, deenergized status, or authorization. No operation, testing sequence, distance, or protective-equipment selection is taught by this dialogue.

C Notice the language

Exact repetition can prevent a dangerous assumption

Similar numbers can sound like a small administrative mistake. Treat the discrepancy as unresolved until the authorized process establishes the correct reference. A respectful repeat-back confirms the message; it does not verify the physical asset or authorize work.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the discrepancy
The work package says Q27; the switching request says Q72.
Keep the hold explicit
The affected task remains on hold.
Avoid choosing by appearance
We have not established which identifier is correct.
Repeat the digits
I read Q two seven in the work package.
Contrast the other reference
The switching request reads Q seven two.
Preserve the documents
Do not silently change either reference.
Name the review owner
Farah will coordinate the authorized reconciliation.
Keep authority separate
This conversation does not authorize an operational action.
Check receipt
Please read back the discrepancy and task status.
Limit the repeat-back
That confirms the message, not the physical asset.
Avoid a state assumption
We cannot infer the equipment's electrical state from these labels.
Check the applicable issue
Reconcile the current documents through revision control.
Maintain role boundaries
Only the relevant authorized personnel can change the operating status.
Avoid an improvised shortcut
Familiarity with the site is not a substitute for verification.
State the next condition
The conflict must be resolved through the required process.
Close accurately
Keep the task on hold until the applicable requirements and authorization are satisfied.
6 language and precision notes
Two seven versus seven two
Read each digit distinctly to expose the mismatch.
Which is correct
An unresolved question, not an invitation to guess.
Remain
Preserves the existing hold instead of implying a new permission.
Coordinate
Describes responsibility for review, not automatic approval.
Does not authorize
Keeps a communication exercise outside operational instruction.
Current
Must be established through revision control, not assumed from a file name.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which message preserves the discrepancy?

Answer and explanation

D. Work package Q27; switching request Q72; correct reference unresolved; task on hold.

The accurate message gives both exact identifiers and preserves the unresolved status and hold.

2. What should happen to the conflicting records?

Answer and explanation

D. Preserve them and use the authorized reconciliation process.

Traceable reconciliation preserves the evidence and avoids inventing a correct identifier.

3. Which conclusion is not supported by a read-back?

Answer and explanation

B. The equipment is safe to work on.

Communication accuracy does not establish electrical state, work safety, or operating authorization.

4. Which response to schedule pressure is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

D. The identifier conflict remains unresolved; the task stays on hold under the local process.

The local hold cannot be replaced by an unverified choice or a language-practice script.

Two identifiers must not be treated as interchangeable

Luis: Field lead / Farah: System operator

  2. Farah
    I have the 1 discrepancy. Keep both references exactly as recorded while I coordinate the authorized review. This conversation does not authorize any operational action.
  3. Luis
    A colleague thinks the digits were simply transposed and wants to correct the request. That may be plausible, but we do not have a verified basis.
  4. Farah
    Do not substitute plausibility for 2. We need the applicable process to establish the correct information and its relationship to the physical asset, not merely make two documents look consistent.
  5. Luis
    I will preserve both documents and report the mismatch. The team knows the site well, but familiarity does not tell us which record is wrong.
  6. Farah
    Correct. The 3 may contain the error, or the associated request may. We must not give either one automatic priority just because it was the first document someone read.
  7. Luis
    Let me read the distinction again: Q two seven in the package; Q seven two in the request. The affected task remains on hold.
  8. Farah
    That 4 confirms the message accurately. It does not verify the physical equipment, establish its electrical state, or satisfy the separate requirements for an authorized operating decision.
  9. Luis
    I will tell the incoming lead you received the mismatch but have not released the task. Both identifiers will stay in the handover.
  10. Farah
    Keep the 5 visible in the handover. My acknowledgement means I received the discrepancy. It is not a release, a clearance, or an instruction to perform switching.
  11. Luis
    Should I label the equipment deenergized? The public outage map shows customers without supply nearby, but that is a different source.
  12. Farah
    Do not infer 6 status from an outage map or these records. The applicable hazardous-energy procedures and qualified verification govern that determination, not a communications summary.
  13. Luis
    I will leave the equipment state unconfirmed. We need to communicate the document problem, not create our own technical work sequence.
  14. Farah
    Exactly. 7 remains with the relevant authorized personnel under the actual procedures. Neither our discussion nor a corrected label alone establishes every condition required for the work.
  15. Luis
    Who coordinates the next review? The incoming lead needs a named contact and a clear statement that the discrepancy remains unresolved.
  16. Farah
    I will coordinate it as the 8 for this exchange, through the authorized process. Keep the current hold and unresolved reference in the handover, and identify any later verified decision by its record.
  17. Luis
    If a replacement file arrives, where will its authorized revision status be recorded? I do not want the crew relying on the filename alone.
  18. Farah
    Use the required 9 process. The relevant records must be reconciled and distributed correctly, with any operational authorization handled under its own requirements rather than implied by an attachment.
  19. Luis

Answers and explanations
  1. equipment identifier Turn 2. The equipment identifier distinguishes the actual asset; Q27 and Q72 cannot be treated as equivalent.
  2. document reconciliation Turn 4. Document reconciliation resolves conflicting references through authorized review, not silent editing to match an assumption.
  3. work package Turn 6. The work package contains task information, but its identifier is not automatically correct merely because it appears first.
  4. read-back Turn 8. Read-back confirms the received identifiers and task status; it does not verify the asset or provide operating permission.
  5. hold point Turn 10. The hold point prevents the affected activity from proceeding until the specified review and authorization requirements have been satisfied.
  6. deenergized Turn 12. Deenergized status requires the applicable hazardous-energy process; an outage map or uncertain label does not establish it.
  7. Operating authority Turn 14. Operating authority belongs to the people assigned the relevant decisions under the actual procedure; this dialogue does not confer it.
  8. system operator Turn 16. The system operator is the named coordinator here; accepting that role does not itself resolve the discrepancy or release the task.
  9. revision control Turn 18. Revision control establishes the applicable document issue and status; a newer-looking file name alone is not sufficient evidence.
  10. switching request Turn 20. A switching request asks for activity; it is not itself a verified authorized instruction to perform that activity.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two identifiers for the same field request

Fictional language simulation: a field request names equipment E12 while its accompanying document names E21. Actual work follows authorized procedures; no switching steps are provided.

  1. Field work coordinator: The request names E12, but the accompanying document names E21. Could you clarify the mismatch?
  2. Operations liaison: I can't identify the correct equipment from those conflicting documents. We need authorized verification.
  3. Field work coordinator: Should I assume it's a typing error and change the identifier that looks less familiar?
  4. Operations liaison: No. Familiarity isn't verification, especially in a switching-order or clearance-related record.
  5. Field work coordinator: I'll request the current approved references and identify both conflicting values without choosing one.
  6. Operations liaison: Please do. Keep any actual work subject to the applicable safety and authorization process.
  7. Field work coordinator: Does receiving a corrected document alone authorize the team to energize the equipment?
  8. Operations liaison: We can't infer that. Document clarification is not a substitute for the required operational authorization.
  9. Field work coordinator: Then our update will say identifier conflict unresolved and verification requested from the responsible authority.
  10. Operations liaison: Correct. This conversation clarifies information; it does not provide lockout, switching, or energizing instructions.
Which document does released refer to?

Separate fictional administrative exercise: an email says released without identifying whether it refers to a document or an operational clearance.

  1. Document coordinator: Your email says released. Could you clarify whether you mean a document issue or a clearance status?
  2. Field planning liaison: I meant the planning document was issued, not that an operational clearance had changed.
  3. Document coordinator: Thank you. That difference needs to appear in the message before I pass it on.
  4. Field planning liaison: I'll revise it to say planning document issued and include its verified reference.
  5. Document coordinator: Does the message establish anything about lockout status or permission to begin the field task?
  6. Field planning liaison: No. Those matters follow the authorized process and are not established by this administrative update.
  7. Document coordinator: I'll ask recipients to repeat the document status if the earlier word caused confusion.
  8. Field planning liaison: Good. We should also preserve the correction so the ambiguous version isn't treated as current.
  9. Document coordinator: I'll distribute the clarified wording through the normal document channel without adding operating instructions.
  10. Field planning liaison: Agreed. Clear language should prevent an administrative milestone being mistaken for operational authority.
A copied order lacks its revision

Separate fictional coordination exercise: a copied switching-order cover sheet has no revision identifier; the complete approved order is unavailable in the conversation.

  1. Scheduling assistant: This copied cover sheet has no revision identifier. Which approved version should the coordination record cite?
  2. Authorized-work liaison: We need document control to verify that. The copy alone doesn't establish the current switching order.
  3. Scheduling assistant: The date looks recent. Would it be reasonable to label it current for the meeting?
  4. Authorized-work liaison: No. A recent date doesn't prove approval or replace a verified revision reference.
  5. Scheduling assistant: I'll request the complete current reference and ask why the identifier is missing from this copy.
  6. Authorized-work liaison: Keep the request neutral. We don't yet know whether the omission occurred during copying or elsewhere.
  7. Scheduling assistant: Should the schedule show the field task as cleared while we wait for the document?
  8. Authorized-work liaison: That status isn't established here. Use the applicable authorization process and keep the document question open.
  9. Scheduling assistant: I'll report the missing revision, verification request, and responsible liaison without inferring clearance.
  10. Authorized-work liaison: Good. The exercise concerns accurate coordination, not instructions to operate or energize equipment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two identifiers must not be treated as interchangeable
  1. Read the corrected dialogue with Q two seven and Q seven two spoken distinctly.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep a correct read-back separate from equipment verification or operating permission.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Lesson 02: Safety, Field Work, and Switching
Original fictional case: A fictional field work package identifies equipment Q27, while its associated switching request identifies Q72. The team has not established which identifier is correct. Field lead Luis has placed the affected task on hold under the local process; no operational action is authorized from these conflicting documents. System operator Farah will coordinate reconciliation through the authorized document and operating procedures. Neither identifier is to be silently corrected, and neither the equipment state nor a safe work condition can be inferred from this dialogue. The language task is to communicate the discrepancy, hold, owner, and next review accurately.
Language workshop: Two identifiers must not be treated as interchangeable
Communication goal: Escalate conflicting equipment references and use exact repeat-back without creating an operational instruction.
Language guidance: Q27 and Q72 contain the same digits in a different order. That resemblance does not prove a harmless typo or identify the correct asset. Keep both references intact in the discrepancy report until authorized reconciliation is complete.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The work package says Q27; the switching request says Q72. / The affected task remains on hold. / We have not established which identifier is correct. / I read Q two seven in the work package. / The switching request reads Q seven two. / Do not silently change either reference. / Farah will coordinate the authorized reconciliation. / This conversation does not authorize an operational action. / Please read back the discrepancy and task status. / That confirms the message, not the physical asset. / We cannot infer the equipment's electrical state from these labels. / Reconcile the current documents through revision control. / Only the relevant authorized personnel can change the operating status. / Familiarity with the site is not a substitute for verification. / The conflict must be resolved through the required process. / Keep the task on hold until the applicable requirements and authorization are satisfied.
Vocabulary: equipment identifier: The exact reference used to identify a particular item of equipment. / asset tag: A label or code assigned to an asset for identification. / work package: The documents and information governing a defined work task. / switching request: A request for specified switching activity through the authorized process. / switching order: An authorized sequence or instruction for switching under the applicable process. / single-line diagram: A simplified diagram representing electrical connections with single lines. / system operator: An authorized person responsible for specified system-operating functions. / field lead: The person leading the relevant field team or task. / energized: Connected to an electrical energy source or otherwise carrying electrical potential as applicable. / deenergized: Without connection to an electrical potential source, stored charge, or voltage relative to earth; not synonymous with safe to work. / isolation: Separation from relevant energy sources under the applicable authorized process. / backfeed: Electrical energy supplied from an unexpected or alternative direction or source. / grounding: Establishing the required electrical connection to ground under applicable procedures. / induced voltage: Voltage produced by electromagnetic influence from another source. / arc flash: A hazardous release of energy associated with an electrical arc. / minimum approach distance: A required separation from electrical hazards under applicable conditions and rules. / qualified worker: A worker with the required training and competence for the specified task. / operating authority: The assigned authority to make or direct specified operational decisions. / clearance: A formal authorization or controlled status with a defined meaning under the operating procedure. / lockout/tagout: Procedures using locks and tags within the applicable hazardous-energy control process. / read-back: Repetition of a received message to check its accuracy. / hold point: A boundary beyond which the affected activity must not proceed until requirements are met. / document reconciliation: Resolving conflicting information through the authorized review and record process. / revision control: Management of document versions and their applicable status.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue with Q two seven and Q seven two spoken distinctly. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep a correct read-back separate from equipment verification or operating permission. Read the checked R18/R81 exchange twice, retaining Ema as coordinator and the task hold.
Partner: Luis: Field lead / Farah: System operator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A work package says R18; its associated request says R81. The correct reference is unresolved. Coordinator Ema owns the review, and the affected task remains on hold.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: If a replacement file arrives, where will its authorized revision status be recorded? I do not want the crew relying on the filename alone.
Farah: Use the required revision control process. The relevant records must be reconciled and distributed correctly, with any operational authorization handled under its own requirements rather than implied by an attachment.
Luis: My summary is Q27 in the package, Q72 in the request, correct identifier unresolved, task on hold, and you coordinating the authorized review.
Farah: That is the right boundary. A switching request is not permission to improvise an operation. Keep the discrepancy and hold explicit until the actual procedure establishes the information and authorization required.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Regulatory Affairs and Rate CasesA proposed rate is mistaken for the current bill+

Your goal

Explain a rate proposal and calculate a conditional bill example without implying approval.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A proposed rate is mistaken for the current bill

A fictional notice proposes increasing an energy charge from $0.10 to $0.105 per kWh, a 5% increase in that component. The monthly fixed charge would remain $20. The proposal is under review; neither approval nor an effective date is established. Customer adviser Hana uses a simplified 500 kWh monthly example with no taxes, credits, or other charges. At the current rates the example totals $70; under the proposal it would total $72.50 at the same usage. Customer Mr Diaz assumes his entire bill has already risen 5%. His actual account has not been reviewed.

Hana: Customer adviser / Mr Diaz: Customer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the proposal's current status?

Answer and explanation

D. Under review, with no approval or effective date established

The supplied notice describes an unapproved proposal with no established effective date.

2. What does the 5% figure describe?

Answer and explanation

D. The energy-charge rate increase from $0.10 to $0.105 per kWh

The percentage applies to the energy rate component, while the fixed charge remains unchanged.

3. What is the current simplified bill at 500 kWh?

Answer and explanation

C. $70

Five hundred times $0.10 is $50, plus the $20 fixed charge gives $70.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The example energy charge rises from $50 to $52.50. With the unchanged $20 fixed charge, the total rises from $70 to $72.50: $2.50, or about 3.57%, not 5%. All proposed figures are conditional.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
tariff
The applicable schedule of rates, terms, and conditions for service.consult the current tariff
rate case
A regulatory proceeding concerning utility revenues, rates, or their allocation.explain the rate case
public utilities commission
A regulatory body overseeing specified utility matters in its jurisdiction.identify the public utilities commission
revenue requirement
The revenue amount considered necessary under the applicable regulatory determination.review the revenue requirement
cost of service
Costs allocated to providing utility service under the relevant analysis.assess cost of service
rate base
The investment value used in the applicable regulatory return calculation.determine the rate base
rate of return
The return rate considered or allowed on the relevant investment basis.explain the rate of return
customer class
A grouping of customers used for service or rate treatment.identify the customer class
fixed charge
A charge not directly varying with the measured usage in the stated example.distinguish the fixed charge
volumetric charge
A charge based on the quantity consumed or delivered.calculate the volumetric charge
demand charge
A charge based on measured or specified demand under the applicable tariff.explain a demand charge
ratepayer
A customer paying the regulated utility's rates.communicate with ratepayers
filing
A formal submission to the relevant authority or proceeding.review a regulatory filing
docket
The official record or identifier for a proceeding.locate the docket
hearing
A formal session for evidence, argument, or public participation.attend a regulatory hearing
effective date
The date on which an approved or applicable provision begins to operate.verify the effective date
proposed rate
A rate submitted for consideration rather than established as current.distinguish the proposed rate
approved rate
A rate accepted through the relevant regulatory process.verify the approved rate
bill impact
The effect of rates and usage on a customer's total charge.illustrate the bill impact
test year
A period used as a basis for the relevant rate-case analysis.identify the test year
rate design
The structure by which charges are assigned and recovered from customers.explain the rate design
rate rider
A separate adjustment or provision attached to the relevant rate structure.identify a rate rider
intervenor
A party participating in a proceeding under its applicable rules.identify an intervenor
usage assumption
The stated consumption level used in a calculation or example.state the usage assumption

The example holds usage at 500 kWh and excludes other charges. It does not predict the actual bill for Mr Diaz. A rate proposal, an approved rate, and a rate in effect are different statuses that must remain distinct.

C Notice the language

Explain the number the customer will actually interpret

A notice may accurately describe a percentage change in one component while a reader hears a change in the whole bill. Identify the component first, then show the conditional total. Do not dismiss the misunderstanding as a failure to read carefully.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the status first
The notice describes a proposal that is still under review.
Avoid implied approval
No approval or effective date is established in this case.
Identify the component
The five percent refers to the energy-charge rate.
State the current rate
The current example rate is ten cents per kilowatt-hour.
State the proposed rate
The proposed rate is ten and a half cents per kilowatt-hour.
Keep the fixed charge
The monthly twenty-dollar fixed charge would remain unchanged.
Give the usage basis
This example holds monthly usage at five hundred kilowatt-hours.
Calculate the current component
The current energy charge is fifty dollars.
Calculate the proposed component
Under the proposal, the energy charge would be fifty-two dollars fifty.
Add the components
The example total would move from seventy dollars to seventy-two dollars fifty.
Distinguish percentage bases
That is about a 3.57 percent increase in this example total.
Preserve the condition
These figures apply only if the proposal is approved as described.
Limit the illustration
Taxes, credits, and other charges are excluded from this simplified example.
Avoid account assumptions
We have not reviewed your actual account or usage.
Offer the relevant record
The official notice and proceeding record show the proposal's status.
Close with a check
The proposal changes one rate component, not every bill by the same percentage.
6 language and precision notes
Would
Keeps proposed charges conditional rather than current.
Per kilowatt-hour
Identifies the usage-based unit in the rate.
At the same usage
Prevents a rate comparison from silently changing consumption.
Percentage of what
Identifies the denominator behind a percentage claim.
In this example
Limits the calculation to its supplied charges and assumptions.
Under review
Neither approved nor necessarily rejected.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What would the proposed energy charge be at 500 kWh?

Answer and explanation

C. $52.50

Five hundred multiplied by $0.105 per kWh gives $52.50 before the fixed charge.

2. What would the simplified total be?

Answer and explanation

D. $72.50

The proposed $52.50 energy charge plus the unchanged $20 fixed charge totals $72.50.

3. Which percentage statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. The energy rate rises 5%; this example total rises about 3.57%.

The unchanged fixed charge means the total's percentage increase differs from the energy-rate increase.

4. Which customer statement preserves the status?

Answer and explanation

B. This is a conditional illustration, not an approved change to your account.

The proposal remains unapproved and the actual account has not been reviewed.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A proposed rate is mistaken for the current bill

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Customer adviser / Mr Diaz: Customer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • bill impact
  • customer class
  • proposed rate
  • docket
  • approved rate
  • usage assumption
  • fixed charge
  • effective date
  • rate design
  • volumetric charge
  1. Mr Diaz
    I received the notice about a five percent increase. Does that mean my whole electricity bill has already gone up by five percent this month?
  2. Hana
    The 1 is still under review. No approval or effective date is established here, so the notice does not mean this change has already reached your bill.
  3. Mr Diaz
    I still want to understand the five percent figure. The notice has several numbers, and I assumed that percentage described my total.
  4. Hana
    It describes the 2 rate, from ten cents to ten and a half cents per kilowatt-hour. The separate monthly fixed charge would remain twenty dollars.
  5. Mr Diaz
    Could you show a simple example of how the energy rate combines with the other charge on a bill?
  6. Hana
    Let's use a 3 of five hundred kilowatt-hours for the month. We will hold that constant and exclude taxes, credits, and other charges so the comparison is clear.
  7. Mr Diaz
    At ten cents for each of five hundred kilowatt-hours, the current energy part is fifty dollars. Then I still add the twenty-dollar monthly charge.
  8. Hana
    Correct. Including the 4, the current example totals seventy dollars. Under the proposal, the energy part would be fifty-two dollars fifty, before that same twenty dollars.
  9. Mr Diaz
    So the proposed example total would be seventy-two dollars fifty. That is two dollars fifty more than seventy, not five percent of the whole amount.
  10. Hana
    Exactly. The 5 in this illustration is about three point five seven percent. The energy rate rises five percent, but the unchanged fixed charge makes the total's percentage different.
  11. Mr Diaz
    My usage is not always five hundred, and my bill has a credit line. Can we check my own account before you estimate my total?
  12. Hana
    Yes. Your 6 and actual applicable terms also matter. We have not reviewed your account, so I should not present this simplified calculation as your personal bill forecast.
  13. Mr Diaz
    Where can I see whether the proposal changes or is accepted? I would prefer the official information rather than a social-media summary of the notice.
  14. Hana
    The official notice and 7 provide the relevant proceeding information. Follow the stated official channels; this fictional example does not supply a real filing number or a participation deadline.
  15. Mr Diaz
    And if a decision is eventually issued, I should still check what it actually approves rather than assume every part of the original request was accepted.
  16. Hana
    Correct. An 8 may differ from what was proposed. The decision and applicable tariff information determine the resulting terms, not the version of the notice first circulated.
  17. Mr Diaz
    If it is approved later, how will I know which bill first uses the new rate? Is that date part of the decision?
  18. Hana
    Check the 9 rather than infer one. Here, neither approval nor that date is established, so the proposed numbers remain conditional throughout our explanation.
  19. Mr Diaz
    I can now explain it accurately: a five percent proposal for one energy-rate component, with a different percentage effect on the simplified total, and no approved change yet.
  20. Hana
    That captures the 10 distinction. Keep the usage and exclusions beside the example, and use would rather than has when describing the proposal's possible effect.

Answers and explanations
  1. proposed rate Turn 2. The proposed rate is under consideration; the notice does not establish approval or a current change to the customer's account.
  2. volumetric charge Turn 4. The volumetric charge depends on consumption; the proposed percentage increase concerns its per-kWh rate, not every component of the total bill.
  3. usage assumption Turn 6. The usage assumption fixes consumption at 500 kWh so the example isolates the proposed rate change rather than changing both rate and usage.
  4. fixed charge Turn 8. The fixed charge is the unchanged $20 component in this example; it must be added to the energy charge to obtain the total.
  5. bill impact Turn 10. Bill impact concerns the total charge; the example's $2.50 increase on $70 is about 3.57%, not the component rate's 5%.
  6. customer class Turn 12. Customer class can determine applicable rate treatment; the simplified example does not establish the terms governing Mr Diaz's actual account.
  7. docket Turn 14. The docket is the proceeding's official record or identifier, allowing the customer to locate the proposal and relevant decisions.
  8. approved rate Turn 16. An approved rate is the outcome accepted through the relevant process; it need not be identical to the original proposal.
  9. effective date Turn 18. The effective date identifies when a provision begins to operate; the supplied case establishes neither that date nor an approval.
  10. rate design Turn 20. Rate design is the structure of charges; separating its components explains why a component-rate percentage differs from the total-bill effect.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr Diaz: I received the notice about a five percent increase. Does that mean my whole electricity bill has already gone up by five percent this month?
  2. Hana: The proposed rate is still under review. No approval or effective date is established here, so the notice does not mean this change has already reached your bill.
