Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A proposed adjustment is not today's tariff

Fictional rate-case communication: a public notice describes a proposed adjustment under review; no approval or effective date is supplied.

  1. Business customer liaison: A customer thinks the proposed rate adjustment already applies to their current bill.
  2. Regulatory communications officer: The notice describes a proposal under review, not an approved change currently in effect.
  3. Business customer liaison: How can I explain a rate case without repeating technical language from the notice?
  4. Regulatory communications officer: Describe it as a review of a requested rate change, with an outcome not yet established.
  5. Business customer liaison: So the proposed tariff wording isn't automatically the schedule used for today's charges?
  6. Regulatory communications officer: Correct. We need the applicable current tariff and account details to discuss an actual bill.
  7. Business customer liaison: Could I reassure the customer that the proposal will certainly be rejected or reduced?
  8. Regulatory communications officer: No. That would predict the review's outcome without evidence and confuse customer impact further.
  9. Business customer liaison: I'll distinguish proposed rates from current charges and refer the bill question for a proper check.
  10. Regulatory communications officer: Good. Keep any effective-date statement limited to what the verified documents actually establish.
A review question is not a finding of imprudence

Separate fictional regulatory briefing: reviewers request more support for a proposed cost; no conclusion about prudence has been issued.

  1. Finance liaison: The reviewers asked for more support. Does that mean they found our spending imprudent?
  2. Regulatory analyst: Not from this request. They are asking for evidence, not stating a final conclusion.
  3. Finance liaison: Could you explain prudence in plain language for the project team attending our briefing?
  4. Regulatory analyst: In this discussion, it concerns whether spending decisions were reasonable under the relevant review framework.
  5. Finance liaison: Then a question about a cost isn't the same as a decision rejecting it.
  6. Regulatory analyst: Exactly. We should describe the requested support and avoid predicting the rate-case outcome.
  7. Finance liaison: Would saying the cost is definitely recoverable be equally unsupported at this stage?
  8. Regulatory analyst: Yes. Neither acceptance nor rejection has been established by the information request we're discussing.
  9. Finance liaison: I'll ask the cost owner for the supporting records and label the issue under review.
  10. Regulatory analyst: That preserves the distinction between a review step and a determination with possible customer impact.
An average impact is not every customer's bill

Separate fictional notice: an illustrative proposal shows an average bill increase of 6 units for a defined sample; individual account effects are not calculated.

  1. Customer programs manager: The illustration says six units more. Can we tell every customer their bill rises by six?
  2. Rate communications analyst: No. That's an average for the defined sample, not a calculation for every account.
  3. Customer programs manager: What familiar explanation would help staff distinguish that average from an individual bill estimate?
  4. Rate communications analyst: An average summarizes the sample; individual charges depend on the applicable terms and account information.
  5. Customer programs manager: And because this is a proposal, the illustration doesn't establish an approved tariff change either?
  6. Rate communications analyst: Correct. Both the proposal status and the sample boundary belong in our explanation.
  7. Customer programs manager: Could we call six units the maximum impact to make the notice sound less concerning?
  8. Rate communications analyst: The figure isn't a maximum. That wording would add a claim the supplied analysis doesn't support.
  9. Customer programs manager: I'll explain the example as illustrative and route individual questions to the proper billing review.
  10. Rate communications analyst: Good. Clear language should retain the distinction, not simplify it into an inaccurate guarantee.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A proposed rate is mistaken for the current bill
  1. Read the corrected exchange with the 500 kWh assumption and unchanged $20 fixed charge.
  2. Switch roles; stress would for $72.50 and distinguish the 5 percent component change from the 3.57 percent total change.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Calculate another conditional example

At 400 kWh, a simplified current bill uses $0.10 per kWh plus a $20 fixed charge. An unapproved proposal would change the energy rate to $0.105. Other charges are excluded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Adviser: "The current total is $___."

Answer and explanation

D. 60

Four hundred times ten cents is forty dollars, plus twenty gives sixty.

2. Customer: "The proposed energy component would be $___."

Answer and explanation

A. 42

Four hundred multiplied by $0.105 gives a forty-two-dollar energy charge.

3. Adviser: "The proposed total would be $___."

Answer and explanation

B. 62

Forty-two dollars plus the unchanged twenty-dollar charge gives sixty-two.

4. Customer: "The proposal remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unapproved

The supplied case explicitly labels the proposed rate as unapproved.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 03: Regulatory Affairs and Rate Cases
Original fictional case: A fictional notice proposes increasing an energy charge from $0.10 to $0.105 per kWh, a 5% increase in that component. The monthly fixed charge would remain $20. The proposal is under review; neither approval nor an effective date is established. Customer adviser Hana uses a simplified 500 kWh monthly example with no taxes, credits, or other charges. At the current rates the example totals $70; under the proposal it would total $72.50 at the same usage. Customer Mr Diaz assumes his entire bill has already risen 5%. His actual account has not been reviewed.
Language workshop: A proposed rate is mistaken for the current bill
Communication goal: Explain a rate proposal and calculate a conditional bill example without implying approval.
Language guidance: The example energy charge rises from $50 to $52.50. With the unchanged $20 fixed charge, the total rises from $70 to $72.50: $2.50, or about 3.57%, not 5%. All proposed figures are conditional.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The notice describes a proposal that is still under review. / No approval or effective date is established in this case. / The five percent refers to the energy-charge rate. / The current example rate is ten cents per kilowatt-hour. / The proposed rate is ten and a half cents per kilowatt-hour. / The monthly twenty-dollar fixed charge would remain unchanged. / This example holds monthly usage at five hundred kilowatt-hours. / The current energy charge is fifty dollars. / Under the proposal, the energy charge would be fifty-two dollars fifty. / The example total would move from seventy dollars to seventy-two dollars fifty. / That is about a 3.57 percent increase in this example total. / These figures apply only if the proposal is approved as described. / Taxes, credits, and other charges are excluded from this simplified example. / We have not reviewed your actual account or usage. / The official notice and proceeding record show the proposal's status. / The proposal changes one rate component, not every bill by the same percentage.
Vocabulary: tariff: The applicable schedule of rates, terms, and conditions for service. / rate case: A regulatory proceeding concerning utility revenues, rates, or their allocation. / public utilities commission: A regulatory body overseeing specified utility matters in its jurisdiction. / revenue requirement: The revenue amount considered necessary under the applicable regulatory determination. / cost of service: Costs allocated to providing utility service under the relevant analysis. / rate base: The investment value used in the applicable regulatory return calculation. / rate of return: The return rate considered or allowed on the relevant investment basis. / customer class: A grouping of customers used for service or rate treatment. / fixed charge: A charge not directly varying with the measured usage in the stated example. / volumetric charge: A charge based on the quantity consumed or delivered. / demand charge: A charge based on measured or specified demand under the applicable tariff. / ratepayer: A customer paying the regulated utility's rates. / filing: A formal submission to the relevant authority or proceeding. / docket: The official record or identifier for a proceeding. / hearing: A formal session for evidence, argument, or public participation. / effective date: The date on which an approved or applicable provision begins to operate. / proposed rate: A rate submitted for consideration rather than established as current. / approved rate: A rate accepted through the relevant regulatory process. / bill impact: The effect of rates and usage on a customer's total charge. / test year: A period used as a basis for the relevant rate-case analysis. / rate design: The structure by which charges are assigned and recovered from customers. / rate rider: A separate adjustment or provision attached to the relevant rate structure. / intervenor: A party participating in a proceeding under its applicable rules. / usage assumption: The stated consumption level used in a calculation or example.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange with the 500 kWh assumption and unchanged $20 fixed charge. Switch roles; stress would for $72.50 and distinguish the 5 percent component change from the 3.57 percent total change. Read the checked transfer twice using $60 current, $42 proposed energy, and $62 proposed total, still unapproved.
Partner: Hana: Customer adviser / Mr Diaz: Customer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 400 kWh, a simplified current bill uses $0.10 per kWh plus a $20 fixed charge. An unapproved proposal would change the energy rate to $0.105. Other charges are excluded.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr Diaz: If it is approved later, how will I know which bill first uses the new rate? Is that date part of the decision?
Hana: Check the effective date rather than infer one. Here, neither approval nor that date is established, so the proposed numbers remain conditional throughout our explanation.
Mr Diaz: I can now explain it accurately: a five percent proposal for one energy-rate component, with a different percentage effect on the simplified total, and no approved change yet.
Hana: That captures the rate design distinction. Keep the usage and exclusions beside the example, and use would rather than has when describing the proposal's possible effect.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Language workshop: A proposed rate is mistaken for the current bill
Communication goal: Explain a rate proposal and calculate a conditional bill example without implying approval.
Language guidance: The example energy charge rises from $50 to $52.50. With the unchanged $20 fixed charge, the total rises from $70 to $72.50: $2.50, or about 3.57%, not 5%. All proposed figures are conditional.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The notice describes a proposal that is still under review. / No approval or effective date is established in this case. / The five percent refers to the energy-charge rate. / The current example rate is ten cents per kilowatt-hour. / The proposed rate is ten and a half cents per kilowatt-hour. / The monthly twenty-dollar fixed charge would remain unchanged. / This example holds monthly usage at five hundred kilowatt-hours. / The current energy charge is fifty dollars. / Under the proposal, the energy charge would be fifty-two dollars fifty. / The example total would move from seventy dollars to seventy-two dollars fifty. / That is about a 3.57 percent increase in this example total. / These figures apply only if the proposal is approved as described. / Taxes, credits, and other charges are excluded from this simplified example. / We have not reviewed your actual account or usage. / The official notice and proceeding record show the proposal's status. / The proposal changes one rate component, not every bill by the same percentage.
Vocabulary: tariff: The applicable schedule of rates, terms, and conditions for service. / rate case: A regulatory proceeding concerning utility revenues, rates, or their allocation. / public utilities commission: A regulatory body overseeing specified utility matters in its jurisdiction. / revenue requirement: The revenue amount considered necessary under the applicable regulatory determination. / cost of service: Costs allocated to providing utility service under the relevant analysis. / rate base: The investment value used in the applicable regulatory return calculation. / rate of return: The return rate considered or allowed on the relevant investment basis. / customer class: A grouping of customers used for service or rate treatment. / fixed charge: A charge not directly varying with the measured usage in the stated example. / volumetric charge: A charge based on the quantity consumed or delivered. / demand charge: A charge based on measured or specified demand under the applicable tariff. / ratepayer: A customer paying the regulated utility's rates. / filing: A formal submission to the relevant authority or proceeding. / docket: The official record or identifier for a proceeding. / hearing: A formal session for evidence, argument, or public participation. / effective date: The date on which an approved or applicable provision begins to operate. / proposed rate: A rate submitted for consideration rather than established as current. / approved rate: A rate accepted through the relevant regulatory process. / bill impact: The effect of rates and usage on a customer's total charge. / test year: A period used as a basis for the relevant rate-case analysis. / rate design: The structure by which charges are assigned and recovered from customers. / rate rider: A separate adjustment or provision attached to the relevant rate structure. / intervenor: A party participating in a proceeding under its applicable rules. / usage assumption: The stated consumption level used in a calculation or example.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange with the 500 kWh assumption and unchanged $20 fixed charge. Switch roles; stress would for $72.50 and distinguish the 5 percent component change from the 3.57 percent total change. Read the checked transfer twice using $60 current, $42 proposed energy, and $62 proposed total, still unapproved.
Partner: Hana: Customer adviser / Mr Diaz: Customer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 400 kWh, a simplified current bill uses $0.10 per kWh plus a $20 fixed charge. An unapproved proposal would change the energy rate to $0.105. Other charges are excluded.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr Diaz: If it is approved later, how will I know which bill first uses the new rate? Is that date part of the decision?
Hana: Check the effective date rather than infer one. Here, neither approval nor that date is established, so the proposed numbers remain conditional throughout our explanation.
Mr Diaz: I can now explain it accurately: a five percent proposal for one energy-rate component, with a different percentage effect on the simplified total, and no approved change yet.
Hana: That captures the rate design distinction. Keep the usage and exclusions beside the example, and use would rather than has when describing the proposal's possible effect.
END REFERENCE
04Renewables Integration and InterconnectionIn the queue does not mean connected+

Your goal

Explain renewable-project review status without confusing capacity, application progress, and operating permission.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

In the queue does not mean connected

A fictional 120 kW rooftop solar project has submitted interconnection application Q314 and received an acknowledgement. It is in the review queue, but inverter data remain incomplete and the metering review is open. No connection approval, permission to operate, confirmed upgrade cost, or operating date has been issued. Developer Elena finds a presentation saying connection secured. Utility coordinator Amir must help replace that claim with the actual status. The 120 kW figure is proposed nameplate power, not annual energy output or a guaranteed export allowance. Applicable utility and jurisdictional requirements govern the real process.

Elena: Project developer / Amir: Utility interconnection coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has the project received?

Answer and explanation

B. An acknowledgement of application Q314

The case establishes submission and acknowledgement, while the substantive reviews remain open.

2. What does 120 kW describe?

Answer and explanation

A. Proposed nameplate power

Kilowatts measure power, and the brief labels this figure as proposed nameplate capacity.

3. Which status is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. Application acknowledged; inverter data incomplete; metering review open

The accurate status preserves both the completed administrative step and the unresolved reviews.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Kilowatts describe power; kilowatt-hours describe energy over time. Proposed nameplate power does not establish annual output, hosting capacity, approved export, or permission to operate. Each needs its own basis and, where relevant, decision.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
interconnection
The arrangement and process for connecting a generating resource to an electrical network.review the interconnection
distributed energy resource (DER)
A generation, storage, or controllable resource connected at the distribution or customer level.assess a distributed energy resource
photovoltaic (PV)
Relating to conversion of sunlight into electricity.develop a photovoltaic project
nameplate capacity
The rated power capacity stated for equipment or a system on its specified basis.state nameplate capacity
inverter
Equipment converting direct-current electricity to alternating-current electricity in the relevant application.submit inverter data
application acknowledgement
Confirmation that a submitted application has been received.distinguish application acknowledgement
review queue
The sequence or grouping of applications awaiting relevant review.track the review queue
queue position
The application's place under the applicable queue rules.confirm queue position
feasibility study
A preliminary assessment of whether and under what conditions a proposed connection may be feasible.review a feasibility study
system impact study
Analysis of a proposed connection's effects on the electrical system.assess the system impact study
facilities study
Review of the facilities and associated work needed for a proposed connection.review the facilities study
hosting capacity
The ability of a network area to accommodate additional resources under defined conditions.assess hosting capacity
export limit
A limit on the power a resource may send to the network under applicable terms.verify the export limit
non-export arrangement
An arrangement intended to prevent export to the network under its specified controls and terms.review a non-export arrangement
protection settings
Parameters governing relevant protective equipment behavior.verify protection settings
metering arrangement
The equipment and terms used to measure relevant flows or usage.confirm the metering arrangement
network upgrade
A change to network facilities required or proposed for a connection or other need.assess network upgrades
cost allocation
Assignment of costs to the relevant parties under the applicable basis.clarify cost allocation
interconnection agreement
The applicable agreement defining the terms of a connection.review the interconnection agreement
permission to operate (PTO)
The required authorization to operate under the relevant utility process.verify permission to operate
commissioning
Verification and documentation of installed-system performance under the relevant process.coordinate commissioning
curtailment
Reduction of output below otherwise available generation under specified conditions.explain curtailment risk
power quality
Characteristics of electrical supply or output relevant to acceptable performance.assess power quality
reactive power
Power associated with alternating-current energy exchange in inductive or capacitive elements.assess reactive-power requirements

An acknowledgement records receipt. A queue position describes an administrative status under applicable rules. Neither supplies the missing inverter data, closes metering review, fixes upgrade costs, or authorizes operation.

C Notice the language

A milestone label can outrun the evidence

A project team may shorten a long status into secured to sound decisive. State the completed administrative step and the open technical questions separately. This allows a positive report of progress without implying a permission or commercial certainty that does not exist.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Report the completed step
Application Q314 has been submitted and acknowledged.
State the current stage
The application is in the review queue.
Correct the slide
Connection secured overstates the current status.
Name the first open item
Inverter data remain incomplete.
Name the second open item
The metering review is still open.
Separate operating permission
No permission to operate has been issued.
Define the capacity figure
The 120 kW figure is proposed nameplate power.
Avoid an energy claim
It is not annual energy output in kilowatt-hours.
Avoid an export assumption
Nameplate capacity does not establish the permitted export limit.
Keep costs qualified
Upgrade costs and their allocation are not confirmed.
Preserve timing uncertainty
No operating date has been issued.
Ask for the applicable route
Which current utility requirements govern this application?
Limit queue language
Queue status does not itself reserve an approved connection.
Coordinate the missing input
We will submit the required inverter information for review.
Avoid automatic completion
Supplying one missing document does not close every review.
Close with separate statuses
Report receipt, technical review, commercial terms, and operating permission separately.
6 language and precision notes
Acknowledged
Means received, not technically approved.
Secured
Can imply a completed right or commitment and is too strong here.
Remain incomplete
Identifies an unresolved information gap.
Nameplate
Describes rated power on its stated basis, not actual energy production.
Not confirmed
Preserves uncertainty about cost or timing.
Separately
Prevents one completed step from being used as evidence for unrelated approvals.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which presentation line is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Q314 acknowledged; technical reviews open; no operating permission issued

The accurate line separates receipt, ongoing review, and the absence of permission.

2. Which unit measures energy rather than power?

Answer and explanation

B. kWh

Kilowatt-hours measure energy; watts, kilowatts, and megawatts measure power.

3. What follows from submitting the missing inverter data?

Answer and explanation

A. That input can be reviewed; other requirements remain separate.

Completing one information submission does not establish outcomes for all technical or commercial matters.

4. Which statement about costs is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Upgrade costs and allocation remain unconfirmed.

The brief explicitly establishes no confirmed upgrade cost or cost-allocation decision.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

In the queue does not mean connected

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Project developer / Amir: Utility interconnection coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • inverter
  • permission to operate
  • network upgrades
  • metering arrangement
  • application acknowledgement
  • export limit
  • cost allocation
  • nameplate capacity
  • interconnection agreement
  • review queue
  1. Elena
    The presentation says connection secured. We have an acknowledgement for Q314, but is that heading too strong?
  2. Amir
    It is. The 1 confirms receipt, not approval. Inverter data remain incomplete, the metering review is open, and no permission to operate has been issued.
  3. Elena
    Can I describe our queue status as capacity reserved, or would that imply a commitment the record does not establish?
  4. Amir
    Describe the 2 status accurately. We have no basis here to claim reserved capacity, an approved export allowance, or a completed connection decision from that administrative step.
  5. Elena
    I will replace secured with application submitted and acknowledged, technical review ongoing. What should I say about the 120 kW figure beside the project name?
  6. Amir
    Label it proposed 3. It describes power on its stated rating basis. It is not annual energy output, which would use kilowatt-hours, and it does not establish actual generation.
  7. Elena
    I will change the label. The slide currently uses the same 120 figure under annual generation, which the rating does not support.
  8. Amir
    Correct. Nor does the figure establish an 4. The permitted operating arrangement depends on the applicable review and terms, not simply the size written on the proposed equipment.
  9. Elena
    The technical team is preparing the missing inverter information. I can give them the action, but I should not promise that supplying it completes every review.
  10. Amir
    Exactly. The 5 data are one open input. The metering review remains separate, and other applicable requirements still need their own verified status under the actual utility process.
  11. Elena
    The investor slide also shows our internal operating target. We have no confirmation that the required reviews will finish by then.
  12. Amir
    Mark it as an internal target, not 6 or a confirmed date. In this case, no operating date has been issued, and no one should interpret the slide as authorization to energize or operate.
  13. Elena
    We should remove the claim that upgrades will cost nothing as well. I do not see any verified study or cost decision supporting that assumption.
  14. Amir
    Yes. Potential 7 and their costs remain unconfirmed here. An application acknowledgement does not determine whether upgrades are needed or which party would pay for them.
  15. Elena
    I will separate the engineering questions from the commercial ones. A technical answer might identify work, but the responsibility for its cost still needs its own basis.
  16. Amir
    That is the 8 distinction. Use the applicable rules and agreements rather than assume that a rooftop project, a queue position, or a proposed budget settles responsibility.
  17. Elena
    What can I put in the next-actions box? The investors need to see which submission our team is preparing and which review remains open.
  18. Amir
    Say the team is submitting the missing data for review while the 9 is being assessed. That is concrete progress, with the open items visible, and does not imply that either review has reached a favorable decision.
  19. Elena
    My revised slide will distinguish proposed power, acknowledged application, open technical items, unconfirmed costs and timing, and the absence of operating permission.
  20. Amir
    Good. The eventual 10 and required operating authorizations each need their actual status reported. Do not let a short heading collapse all those stages into one unsupported claim.

Answers and explanations
  1. application acknowledgement Turn 2. Application acknowledgement confirms receipt of Q314, not connection approval or permission to operate.
  2. review queue Turn 4. The review queue identifies an administrative stage, not reserved capacity or an approved connection.
  3. nameplate capacity Turn 6. Nameplate capacity is rated power on a stated basis, not annual energy or an approved export allowance.
  4. export limit Turn 8. An export limit governs power sent to the network; the proposed nameplate rating does not establish it.
  5. inverter Turn 10. Inverter data are one outstanding input; submitting them does not automatically close metering review or other requirements.
  6. permission to operate Turn 12. Permission to operate is the relevant required authorization; an internal target date does not supply it.
  7. network upgrades Turn 14. Network upgrades are changes to network facilities; their scope and cost are unconfirmed here.
  8. cost allocation Turn 16. Cost allocation assigns costs to parties; identifying technical work does not itself settle who pays.
  9. metering arrangement Turn 18. The metering arrangement remains under review, separate from the completed administrative receipt of the application.
  10. interconnection agreement Turn 20. The interconnection agreement sets connection terms, distinct from the application and any separate operating requirements.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An application is not an approved connection

Fictional renewable-project review: an interconnection application is submitted, while technical and administrative reviews remain open.

  1. Project developer: Our slide says approved connection because the interconnection application has entered the queue.
  2. Interconnection coordinator: Queue entry establishes application progress, not approval. Technical and administrative reviews are still open.
  3. Project developer: Then what milestone can we accurately communicate to the investment team this afternoon?
  4. Interconnection coordinator: Say the application is submitted and under review, with connection approval not yet established.
  5. Project developer: Does the requested capacity establish how much the project will be allowed to export?
  6. Interconnection coordinator: Not from the application alone. We haven't received the authorized outcome or applicable conditions.
  7. Project developer: Should we also avoid assuming that no curtailment conditions will apply to this project?
  8. Interconnection coordinator: Yes. The supplied information doesn't establish those conditions, so keep them unresolved.
  9. Project developer: I'll revise the milestone and list the review questions rather than imply a granted connection.
  10. Interconnection coordinator: Good. The next update can report verified decisions without turning an application into operating permission.
A preliminary study does not settle capacity

Separate fictional case: a preliminary interconnection study models one project configuration; a proposed larger configuration has not been studied.

  1. Renewables project manager: The preliminary study looks favorable. Can we use it to support the larger configuration too?
  2. Grid studies liaison: It covers the original configuration only. The increased capacity hasn't been assessed in that study.
  3. Renewables project manager: The site is unchanged, so I thought the result would transfer without further questions.
  4. Grid studies liaison: An unchanged site doesn't establish that different capacity has the same assessed effects.
  5. Renewables project manager: Then our statement should name the studied configuration and keep the larger proposal unassessed.
  6. Grid studies liaison: Exactly. Also retain preliminary status instead of presenting the study as a final interconnection approval.
  7. Renewables project manager: Could we ask the responsible team what review the revised application would require?
  8. Grid studies liaison: Yes. That's a bounded request, without assuming queue treatment, technical acceptance, or operating conditions.
  9. Renewables project manager: I'll separate the existing study result from the proposal and request that clarification.
  10. Grid studies liaison: Good. That keeps the evidence useful without extending it beyond the configuration actually reviewed.
A queue position is not a guaranteed date

Separate fictional project meeting: a queue record lists a project position but no confirmed connection date; the team wants to announce energization timing.

  1. Development coordinator: We have a queue position now. Can the announcement give a definite connection date?
  2. Interconnection analyst: The queue record doesn't supply one. A position isn't evidence of a guaranteed completion date.
  3. Development coordinator: Could we estimate the date from the project immediately ahead of ours in the list?
  4. Interconnection analyst: We haven't established comparable review requirements or conditions, so that comparison wouldn't confirm our timing.
  5. Development coordinator: What positive progress can we report without giving the impression that nothing has happened?
  6. Interconnection analyst: Report the recorded queue milestone and clearly state that reviews and connection timing remain unresolved.
  7. Development coordinator: Should we describe the requested capacity as reserved for us until those reviews finish?
  8. Interconnection analyst: Not unless the relevant verified documents establish that status. This queue entry doesn't do so here.
  9. Development coordinator: I'll remove the energization date and ask the responsible team what timing information is available.
  10. Interconnection analyst: That is accurate progress reporting, not a promise of technical approval or permission to connect.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 04: Renewables Integration and Interconnection
Original fictional case: A fictional 120 kW rooftop solar project has submitted interconnection application Q314 and received an acknowledgement. It is in the review queue, but inverter data remain incomplete and the metering review is open. No connection approval, permission to operate, confirmed upgrade cost, or operating date has been issued. Developer Elena finds a presentation saying connection secured. Utility coordinator Amir must help replace that claim with the actual status. The 120 kW figure is proposed nameplate power, not annual energy output or a guaranteed export allowance. Applicable utility and jurisdictional requirements govern the real process.
Language workshop: In the queue does not mean connected
Communication goal: Explain renewable-project review status without confusing capacity, application progress, and operating permission.
Language guidance: Kilowatts describe power; kilowatt-hours describe energy over time. Proposed nameplate power does not establish annual output, hosting capacity, approved export, or permission to operate. Each needs its own basis and, where relevant, decision.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Application Q314 has been submitted and acknowledged. / The application is in the review queue. / Connection secured overstates the current status. / Inverter data remain incomplete. / The metering review is still open. / No permission to operate has been issued. / The 120 kW figure is proposed nameplate power. / It is not annual energy output in kilowatt-hours. / Nameplate capacity does not establish the permitted export limit. / Upgrade costs and their allocation are not confirmed. / No operating date has been issued. / Which current utility requirements govern this application? / Queue status does not itself reserve an approved connection. / We will submit the required inverter information for review. / Supplying one missing document does not close every review. / Report receipt, technical review, commercial terms, and operating permission separately.
Vocabulary: interconnection: The arrangement and process for connecting a generating resource to an electrical network. / distributed energy resource (DER): A generation, storage, or controllable resource connected at the distribution or customer level. / photovoltaic (PV): Relating to conversion of sunlight into electricity. / nameplate capacity: The rated power capacity stated for equipment or a system on its specified basis. / inverter: Equipment converting direct-current electricity to alternating-current electricity in the relevant application. / application acknowledgement: Confirmation that a submitted application has been received. / review queue: The sequence or grouping of applications awaiting relevant review. / queue position: The application's place under the applicable queue rules. / feasibility study: A preliminary assessment of whether and under what conditions a proposed connection may be feasible. / system impact study: Analysis of a proposed connection's effects on the electrical system. / facilities study: Review of the facilities and associated work needed for a proposed connection. / hosting capacity: The ability of a network area to accommodate additional resources under defined conditions. / export limit: A limit on the power a resource may send to the network under applicable terms. / non-export arrangement: An arrangement intended to prevent export to the network under its specified controls and terms. / protection settings: Parameters governing relevant protective equipment behavior. / metering arrangement: The equipment and terms used to measure relevant flows or usage. / network upgrade: A change to network facilities required or proposed for a connection or other need. / cost allocation: Assignment of costs to the relevant parties under the applicable basis. / interconnection agreement: The applicable agreement defining the terms of a connection. / permission to operate (PTO): The required authorization to operate under the relevant utility process. / commissioning: Verification and documentation of installed-system performance under the relevant process. / curtailment: Reduction of output below otherwise available generation under specified conditions. / power quality: Characteristics of electrical supply or output relevant to acceptable performance. / reactive power: Power associated with alternating-current energy exchange in inductive or capacitive elements.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation, labeling 120 kW as proposed nameplate power. Switch roles for turns 11-20; separate an internal target, upgrade cost allocation, and operating permission. Read the checked Z206 transfer twice with 75 kW and the open metering review. Do not invent annual generation.
Partner: Elena: Project developer / Amir: Utility interconnection coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A proposed 75 kW project has acknowledgement for application Z206. Metering review is open, and no permission to operate is issued.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: What can I put in the next-actions box? The investors need to see which submission our team is preparing and which review remains open.
Amir: Say the team is submitting the missing data for review while the metering arrangement is being assessed. That is concrete progress, with the open items visible, and does not imply that either review has reached a favorable decision.
Elena: My revised slide will distinguish proposed power, acknowledged application, open technical items, unconfirmed costs and timing, and the absence of operating permission.
Amir: Good. The eventual interconnection agreement and required operating authorizations each need their actual status reported. Do not let a short heading collapse all those stages into one unsupported claim.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Asset Management and MaintenanceThe cheapest purchase is not the cheapest comparison+

Your goal

Compare a stated cost basis, test an assumption, and keep the purchase decision separate from partial arithmetic.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The cheapest purchase is not the cheapest comparison

Two fictional asset options are compared over ten years. Option A costs $10,000 to purchase and $2,000 per year in maintenance. Option B costs $14,000 to purchase and $1,000 per year in maintenance. The exercise uses constant annual amounts and no discounting. Installation, energy, downtime, disposal, residual value, and technical equivalence have not been assessed. Analyst Sam finds a slide calling A the lowest-cost choice because its purchase price is lower. Asset manager Priya requests a purchase-and-maintenance comparison, with its limits stated. Neither option is approved for purchase.

Sam: Asset analyst / Priya: Asset manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Option A's ten-year purchase-and-maintenance total?

Answer and explanation

C. $30,000

Ten years of $2,000 maintenance plus the $10,000 purchase equals $30,000.

2. What is Option B's corresponding total?

Answer and explanation

C. $24,000

Ten years of $1,000 maintenance plus the $14,000 purchase equals $24,000.

3. What does the supplied comparison establish?

Answer and explanation

B. B is $6,000 lower on this limited undiscounted basis.

The arithmetic favors B on the stated basis but excludes several costs and technical questions.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A totals $30,000 and B totals $24,000 on the supplied purchase-and-maintenance basis. B is $6,000 lower despite its $4,000 higher purchase price. Neither number is a complete life-cycle estimate.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
asset management
Coordinating asset decisions across performance, risk, cost, and life.develop an asset-management plan
purchase price
The amount paid to acquire an asset.compare purchase prices
capital expenditure (CAPEX)
Spending on acquiring or improving long-lived assets under the applicable accounting treatment.assess capital expenditure
operating expenditure (OPEX)
Costs of operating the business or assets under the relevant accounting treatment.forecast operating expenditure
preventive maintenance
Planned maintenance intended to reduce failures or deterioration.schedule preventive maintenance
corrective maintenance
Maintenance performed to address an identified fault or failure.track corrective maintenance
condition-based maintenance
Maintenance guided by evidence of the actual asset condition.evaluate condition-based maintenance
maintenance interval
The period or usage between planned maintenance actions.review the maintenance interval
recurring cost
A cost expected to occur repeatedly over a period.estimate recurring costs
assessment horizon
The period over which options are evaluated.define the assessment horizon
life-cycle cost
Cost across the relevant stages of an asset's life on a defined basis.assess life-cycle cost
total cost of ownership
The included acquisition, operation, maintenance, and other ownership costs over a stated period.compare total cost of ownership
downtime
Time during which an asset is unavailable for its intended service.account for downtime
failure mode
A particular way an asset or component can fail.assess a failure mode
asset criticality
The importance of an asset based on the consequences of its failure or unavailability.assess asset criticality
remaining useful life
The estimated period of useful service still available from an asset.estimate remaining useful life
replacement cycle
The timing or pattern used to replace assets.plan the replacement cycle
residual value
The value remaining at the end of the assessment period.estimate residual value
disposal cost
The cost of removing or disposing of an asset at the relevant stage.include disposal costs
discounted cash flow
Evaluation of future cash flows using an explicit discount-rate basis.distinguish discounted cash flow
sensitivity analysis
Testing how a result changes when an assumption changes.run a sensitivity analysis
technical equivalence
Comparable ability to meet the specified technical requirements.establish technical equivalence
cost boundary
The set of costs included or excluded from an analysis.state the cost boundary
decision criteria
The factors used to assess and choose among alternatives.agree decision criteria

If B maintenance were $1,600 per year instead, its ten-year total would be $30,000, equal to A on this limited basis. That sensitivity result changes no technical finding and does not approve either purchase.

C Notice the language

Challenge the comparison, not the budget owner

A low initial price can be attractive when a team faces a tight annual budget. Show which costs the slide includes before challenging its conclusion. A transparent comparison lets colleagues discuss affordability and long-term cost as related but different questions.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the narrow comparison
The slide compares purchase prices only.
State the higher upfront amount
B costs four thousand dollars more to purchase.
Name the horizon
We are comparing ten years.
Explain the recurring cost
A has two thousand dollars of annual maintenance.
Calculate A
Ten thousand plus ten times two thousand gives thirty thousand dollars.
Calculate B
Fourteen thousand plus ten times one thousand gives twenty-four thousand dollars.
State the limited result
B is six thousand dollars lower on this purchase-and-maintenance basis.
Preserve the exclusions
Installation, energy, downtime, disposal, and residual value are excluded.
Avoid a broad label
This is not a complete life-cycle-cost comparison.
Name the financial assumption
The figures are undiscounted and use constant annual amounts.
Check technical suitability
Technical equivalence remains unassessed.
Test an assumption
At sixteen hundred dollars a year, B's maintenance changes the comparison.
State the sensitivity result
B would then total thirty thousand dollars over ten years.
Separate affordability
A lower upfront price and a lower long-term total are different considerations.
Avoid an approval claim
Neither option is approved for purchase.
Close with the decision basis
Present the arithmetic, exclusions, and unresolved decision criteria together.
6 language and precision notes
On this basis
Limits a result to the included costs and assumptions.
Upfront
Refers to the initial expenditure, not the total over time.
Per year
Must be multiplied by the stated number of years.
Undiscounted
Does not adjust future amounts to a present-value basis.
Would then
Marks an alternative assumption rather than the original estimate.
Unassessed
Keeps technical suitability separate from the cost calculation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Why is lowest-cost choice unsupported for A?

Answer and explanation

D. It uses purchase price while ignoring the supplied recurring maintenance difference.

A is cheaper upfront, but the supplied ten-year maintenance comparison favors B.

2. At $1,600 annual maintenance, what would B total over ten years?

Answer and explanation

C. $30,000

Fourteen thousand plus ten times sixteen hundred equals thirty thousand dollars.

3. Which label fits the original calculation?

Answer and explanation

A. Ten-year undiscounted purchase-and-maintenance comparison

The calculation includes only the stated purchase and maintenance amounts without discounting.

4. What remains necessary before a full decision?

Answer and explanation

C. Assess omitted costs, technical suitability, and the relevant decision criteria.

The limited arithmetic leaves material financial and technical matters unresolved.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The cheapest purchase is not the cheapest comparison

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sam: Asset analyst / Priya: Asset manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • decision criteria
  • purchase price
  • Technical equivalence
  • Total cost of ownership
  • assessment horizon
  • discounted cash flow
  • cost boundary
  • sensitivity analysis
  • recurring cost
  • residual value
  1. Sam
    A costs ten thousand upfront and B fourteen thousand. The slide calls A the lowest-cost choice. Is that too broad?
  2. Priya
    Yes. It compares 1, not the full cost basis. Both options have recurring maintenance, and their annual amounts differ enough to change the longer comparison.
  3. Sam
    A needs two thousand dollars a year in maintenance; B needs one thousand. We are using ten years and constant annual figures, without discounting.
  4. Priya
    Keep that 2 visible. For A, ten thousand plus ten times two thousand gives thirty thousand dollars. We should not compare ten years of one option with one year of another.
  5. Sam
    B totals twenty-four thousand: fourteen thousand plus ten times one thousand. That is six thousand lower on this basis.
  6. Priya
    Correct. The 3 reverses the purchase-price ranking. But describe that as a purchase-and-maintenance result, not proof that B is the best asset for every purpose.
  7. Sam
    The calculation leaves out installation, energy, downtime, disposal, and residual value. We also have not established that both options satisfy the technical requirements equally.
  8. Priya
    State the 4 beside the totals. An omitted cost is not automatically zero. The audience should see what this comparison answers and what still requires assessment.
  9. Sam
    I will label this purchase and maintenance, not complete ownership cost.
  10. Priya
    Exactly. 5 needs an explicit scope and period. Our figures cover only two categories, so we should not imply that all relevant ownership effects have been evaluated.
  11. Sam
    The maintenance forecast is uncertain. Could we test a different amount for B to show how sensitive the result is to that assumption?
  12. Priya
    Use a 6. At sixteen hundred dollars a year, B's ten-year maintenance is sixteen thousand, bringing its total with purchase to thirty thousand dollars.
  13. Sam
    That would match A on this limited basis. It would not prove the two options have identical performance, risks, or costs outside the comparison.
  14. Priya
    Correct. 7 remains unassessed. A matching subtotal does not tell us whether either option meets the required duty, reliability, operating conditions, or other technical criteria.
  15. Sam
    We also have no residual-value estimate. One asset might retain more value at the end of ten years, but we cannot assume an amount.
  16. Priya
    Keep 8 among the exclusions. The same applies to disposal costs and downtime. We need their actual basis before including them in a wider analysis.
  17. Sam
    The heading says present value, but we have only added the amounts. I will need to correct that heading too, unless a separate discounted analysis is available.
  18. Priya
    No. This is not 9. We have not applied a discount rate. Use undiscounted purchase-and-maintenance comparison so the heading does not promise an analysis we did not perform.
  19. Sam
    I will show A at thirty thousand and B at twenty-four thousand, with the alternative B-maintenance case, exclusions, and no purchase approval.
  20. Priya
    Good. Put the unresolved 10 beside those figures. Affordability, technical suitability, risk, and the wider cost basis remain part of the actual decision, not assumptions hidden by a low price.

Answers and explanations
  1. purchase price Turn 2. Purchase price is the acquisition amount only; the slide incorrectly uses it to describe the entire cost choice.
  2. assessment horizon Turn 4. The assessment horizon is the ten-year period used to multiply recurring annual costs and compare the options.
  3. recurring cost Turn 6. The recurring maintenance cost accumulates over the ten-year period and reverses the ranking based on purchase price alone.
  4. cost boundary Turn 8. The cost boundary identifies included and excluded costs, preventing the partial comparison from appearing to cover all ownership effects.
  5. Total cost of ownership Turn 10. Total cost of ownership requires an explicit scope of relevant ownership costs; this exercise supplies only purchase and maintenance.
  6. sensitivity analysis Turn 12. Sensitivity analysis changes an assumption to test its effect; here it examines a higher annual maintenance amount for B.
  7. Technical equivalence Turn 14. Technical equivalence concerns ability to meet the required performance, which financial equality on a limited basis does not establish.
  8. residual value Turn 16. Residual value is the value remaining at the assessment horizon; it is excluded and has not been estimated here.
  9. discounted cash flow Turn 18. Discounted cash flow uses an explicit discount-rate basis for future amounts; the supplied arithmetic deliberately does not do that.
  10. decision criteria Turn 20. Decision criteria include the factors needed for the actual choice; limited cost arithmetic does not settle every financial or technical consideration.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: A costs ten thousand upfront and B fourteen thousand. The slide calls A the lowest-cost choice. Is that too broad?
  2. Priya: Yes. It compares purchase price, not the full cost basis. Both options have recurring maintenance, and their annual amounts differ enough to change the longer comparison.
  3. Sam: A needs two thousand dollars a year in maintenance; B needs one thousand. We are using ten years and constant annual figures, without discounting.
  4. Priya: Keep that assessment horizon visible. For A, ten thousand plus ten times two thousand gives thirty thousand dollars. We should not compare ten years of one option with one year of another.
  5. Sam: B totals twenty-four thousand: fourteen thousand plus ten times one thousand. That is six thousand lower on this basis.
  6. Priya: Correct. The recurring cost reverses the purchase-price ranking. But describe that as a purchase-and-maintenance result, not proof that B is the best asset for every purpose.
  7. Sam: The calculation leaves out installation, energy, downtime, disposal, and residual value. We also have not established that both options satisfy the technical requirements equally.
  8. Priya: State the cost boundary beside the totals. An omitted cost is not automatically zero. The audience should see what this comparison answers and what still requires assessment.
  9. Sam: I will label this purchase and maintenance, not complete ownership cost.
  10. Priya: Exactly. Total cost of ownership needs an explicit scope and period. Our figures cover only two categories, so we should not imply that all relevant ownership effects have been evaluated.
  11. Sam: The maintenance forecast is uncertain. Could we test a different amount for B to show how sensitive the result is to that assumption?
  12. Priya: Use a sensitivity analysis. At sixteen hundred dollars a year, B's ten-year maintenance is sixteen thousand, bringing its total with purchase to thirty thousand dollars.
  13. Sam: That would match A on this limited basis. It would not prove the two options have identical performance, risks, or costs outside the comparison.
  14. Priya: Correct. Technical equivalence remains unassessed. A matching subtotal does not tell us whether either option meets the required duty, reliability, operating conditions, or other technical criteria.
  15. Sam: We also have no residual-value estimate. One asset might retain more value at the end of ten years, but we cannot assume an amount.
  16. Priya: Keep residual value among the exclusions. The same applies to disposal costs and downtime. We need their actual basis before including them in a wider analysis.
  17. Sam: The heading says present value, but we have only added the amounts. I will need to correct that heading too, unless a separate discounted analysis is available.
  18. Priya: No. This is not discounted cash flow. We have not applied a discount rate. Use undiscounted purchase-and-maintenance comparison so the heading does not promise an analysis we did not perform.
  19. Sam: I will show A at thirty thousand and B at twenty-four thousand, with the alternative B-maintenance case, exclusions, and no purchase approval.

Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Purchase price leaves out maintenance

Fictional asset comparison: option A costs 40 units plus an estimated 12 maintenance units over five years; B costs 48 plus 3. Other costs and performance are unassessed.

  1. Budget manager: Option A costs forty and B costs forty-eight. Why isn't A clearly the cheaper asset?
  2. Asset analyst: For the same five-year period, estimated maintenance is twelve for A and three for B.
  3. Budget manager: So the listed purchase-plus-maintenance totals are fifty-two and fifty-one, respectively, under these assumptions.
  4. Asset analyst: Correct, but those aren't complete lifecycle costs. We haven't assessed other costs or performance.
  5. Budget manager: Can we still recommend B solely because its listed total is one unit lower?
  6. Asset analyst: That difference alone isn't a full decision basis. We need comparable condition and performance information.
  7. Budget manager: What tradeoff should the slide show while we obtain the missing assessment?
  8. Asset analyst: A has lower initial cost; B has lower estimated maintenance over the same stated period.
  9. Budget manager: I'll use identical categories and label missing costs, rather than call either total comprehensive.
  10. Asset analyst: Good. The comparison can inform asset management without pretending our incomplete estimates settle the choice.
Different observation periods distort failure comparisons

Separate fictional asset review: asset group A has two failures over one year; group B has three over three years. Group sizes and operating exposure are not supplied.

  1. Maintenance manager: Group B had three failures and A had two. Should we call B less reliable?
  2. Condition analyst: The observation periods differ, and we don't have comparable group sizes or operating exposure.
  3. Maintenance manager: Could I simply divide B's count by three and use that as a failure rate?
  4. Condition analyst: That gives a yearly count, but still doesn't resolve the missing exposure and population information.
  5. Maintenance manager: Then we need the same comparison criteria before making an asset-management recommendation from these figures.
  6. Condition analyst: Yes. Include time, population, relevant use, and condition assessment alongside the recorded events.
  7. Maintenance manager: The budget team wants a quick choice. Can we at least rank the groups provisionally?
  8. Condition analyst: We can show the raw observations with their limitations, but shouldn't present an unsupported reliability ranking.
  9. Maintenance manager: I'll request the missing denominators and describe why the present counts aren't directly comparable.
  10. Condition analyst: That preserves the evidence and prevents an apparent difference becoming an unjustified investment conclusion.
Remote monitoring versus inspection visits

Separate fictional planning comparison: two proposed asset-monitoring packages cover the same twelve assets; A has more visits, B has remote reports. Costs and suitability await qualified review.

  1. Asset program coordinator: Package A includes more site visits; B provides remote reports for the same twelve assets.
  2. Maintenance planning lead: Which criteria will we use to compare them beyond the number of visits included?
  3. Asset program coordinator: Coverage, information quality, cost, and required follow-up should be assessed consistently for both packages.
  4. Maintenance planning lead: Can we assume remote reports eliminate the need for any onsite condition assessment?
  5. Asset program coordinator: No. The package description doesn't establish that, and technical suitability needs the appropriate review.
  6. Maintenance planning lead: Then the tradeoff is different ways of collecting information, not automatically better safety or reliability.
  7. Asset program coordinator: Exactly. We also need to know what each package excludes before comparing lifecycle cost.
  8. Maintenance planning lead: I'll request the exclusions and follow-up responsibilities from both suppliers using the same questions.
  9. Asset program coordinator: Good. Keep the same twelve-asset scope in both responses so the comparison remains meaningful.
  10. Maintenance planning lead: We'll present the verified differences and unresolved suitability questions rather than announce a winner prematurely.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The cheapest purchase is not the cheapest comparison
  1. Read the corrected dialogue with $30,000 for A and $24,000 for B over ten years.
  2. Switch roles; explain the $1,600 annual-maintenance sensitivity without implying technical equivalence.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Compare the same included costs

Over five years, C costs $8,000 plus $1,000 annual maintenance. D costs $10,000 plus $400 annual maintenance. Use undiscounted purchase and maintenance only.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "C totals $___."

Answer and explanation

D. 13,000

Eight thousand plus five years at one thousand equals thirteen thousand.

2. Manager: "D totals $___."

Answer and explanation

B. 12,000

Ten thousand plus five years at four hundred equals twelve thousand.

3. Analyst: "D is $___ lower on this basis."

Answer and explanation

A. 1,000

The difference between thirteen thousand and twelve thousand is one thousand.

4. Manager: "The comparison excludes ___ costs."

Answer and explanation

D. other

The setup includes purchase and maintenance only, leaving other cost categories outside the calculation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 05: Asset Management and Maintenance
Original fictional case: Two fictional asset options are compared over ten years. Option A costs $10,000 to purchase and $2,000 per year in maintenance. Option B costs $14,000 to purchase and $1,000 per year in maintenance. The exercise uses constant annual amounts and no discounting. Installation, energy, downtime, disposal, residual value, and technical equivalence have not been assessed. Analyst Sam finds a slide calling A the lowest-cost choice because its purchase price is lower. Asset manager Priya requests a purchase-and-maintenance comparison, with its limits stated. Neither option is approved for purchase.
Language workshop: The cheapest purchase is not the cheapest comparison
Communication goal: Compare a stated cost basis, test an assumption, and keep the purchase decision separate from partial arithmetic.
Language guidance: A totals $30,000 and B totals $24,000 on the supplied purchase-and-maintenance basis. B is $6,000 lower despite its $4,000 higher purchase price. Neither number is a complete life-cycle estimate.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The slide compares purchase prices only. / B costs four thousand dollars more to purchase. / We are comparing ten years. / A has two thousand dollars of annual maintenance. / Ten thousand plus ten times two thousand gives thirty thousand dollars. / Fourteen thousand plus ten times one thousand gives twenty-four thousand dollars. / B is six thousand dollars lower on this purchase-and-maintenance basis. / Installation, energy, downtime, disposal, and residual value are excluded. / This is not a complete life-cycle-cost comparison. / The figures are undiscounted and use constant annual amounts. / Technical equivalence remains unassessed. / At sixteen hundred dollars a year, B's maintenance changes the comparison. / B would then total thirty thousand dollars over ten years. / A lower upfront price and a lower long-term total are different considerations. / Neither option is approved for purchase. / Present the arithmetic, exclusions, and unresolved decision criteria together.
Vocabulary: asset management: Coordinating asset decisions across performance, risk, cost, and life. / purchase price: The amount paid to acquire an asset. / capital expenditure (CAPEX): Spending on acquiring or improving long-lived assets under the applicable accounting treatment. / operating expenditure (OPEX): Costs of operating the business or assets under the relevant accounting treatment. / preventive maintenance: Planned maintenance intended to reduce failures or deterioration. / corrective maintenance: Maintenance performed to address an identified fault or failure. / condition-based maintenance: Maintenance guided by evidence of the actual asset condition. / maintenance interval: The period or usage between planned maintenance actions. / recurring cost: A cost expected to occur repeatedly over a period. / assessment horizon: The period over which options are evaluated. / life-cycle cost: Cost across the relevant stages of an asset's life on a defined basis. / total cost of ownership: The included acquisition, operation, maintenance, and other ownership costs over a stated period. / downtime: Time during which an asset is unavailable for its intended service. / failure mode: A particular way an asset or component can fail. / asset criticality: The importance of an asset based on the consequences of its failure or unavailability. / remaining useful life: The estimated period of useful service still available from an asset. / replacement cycle: The timing or pattern used to replace assets. / residual value: The value remaining at the end of the assessment period. / disposal cost: The cost of removing or disposing of an asset at the relevant stage. / discounted cash flow: Evaluation of future cash flows using an explicit discount-rate basis. / sensitivity analysis: Testing how a result changes when an assumption changes. / technical equivalence: Comparable ability to meet the specified technical requirements. / cost boundary: The set of costs included or excluded from an analysis. / decision criteria: The factors used to assess and choose among alternatives.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue with $30,000 for A and $24,000 for B over ten years. Switch roles; explain the $1,600 annual-maintenance sensitivity without implying technical equivalence. Read the checked C/D exchange twice. Keep the five-year, undiscounted, purchase-and-maintenance basis explicit.
Partner: Sam: Asset analyst / Priya: Asset manager

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
