06Emergency Preparedness and Storm ResponseRequested resources are not all available+

Your goal

Hand over storm-response resources with exact counts, distinct statuses, and a clear receiving owner.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Requested resources are not all available

At the 19:30 local coordination handover, six external crews have been requested. Three are confirmed: one is at the staging location and two are en route. The other three remain unconfirmed. Twenty lodging spaces were requested; twelve are confirmed and eight are pending. Arrival times for the two traveling crews are not verified. Outgoing coordinator Daniel hands over to Sofia, who accepts tracking the open requests and providing the next status update at 20:00. Confirmed, arrived, briefed, and authorized for deployment are separate stages; this record establishes no new operational assignment or restoration promise.

Daniel: Outgoing logistics coordinator / Sofia: Incoming logistics coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many requested crews are confirmed?

Answer and explanation

D. Three

The brief confirms three of the six requested crews, with three still unconfirmed.

2. How many of the confirmed crews have arrived at staging?

Answer and explanation

C. One

One confirmed crew is at staging; the other two confirmed crews are en route.

3. How many lodging spaces remain pending?

Answer and explanation

A. Eight

Twenty were requested and twelve confirmed, leaving eight spaces pending.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The one arrived crew and two en-route crews are subsets of the three confirmed crews. They are not three additional crews. Six requested minus three confirmed leaves three requests unconfirmed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
emergency operations center (EOC)
A location or function coordinating information and resources during an emergency.support the emergency operations center
incident command
The authority structure directing an incident response under the applicable system.coordinate with incident command
mutual assistance
Support provided by other organizations under applicable arrangements.request mutual assistance
resource request
A documented request for specified people, equipment, or support.track a resource request
resource confirmation
Verification that the requested resource is committed on stated terms.obtain resource confirmation
staging location
A designated place where resources assemble before further assignment.report arrival at staging
mobilization
Preparing and moving resources for an assigned response.track mobilization status
demobilization
Organized release and return of resources when no longer required.plan demobilization
en route
Traveling toward the relevant destination, not yet arrived.report crews en route
estimated time of arrival (ETA)
An estimate of when a person or resource will reach a destination.verify the estimated time of arrival
deployment authorization
Permission to assign or send resources to specified work under the applicable process.verify deployment authorization
crew roster
A list of personnel assigned to a crew or response.reconcile the crew roster
credential verification
Checking the relevant qualifications or authorizations of personnel.complete credential verification
shift handover
Transfer of current information and responsibilities between shifts.conduct a shift handover
operational period
A defined interval used for response planning and coordination.identify the operational period
situation report
A structured update of current conditions, actions, and resource status.issue a situation report
logistics
Coordination of resources, movement, accommodation, and other support.coordinate response logistics
lodging allocation
Assignment of accommodation capacity to the relevant personnel.confirm lodging allocation
resource shortfall
The gap between required or requested resources and those available or confirmed.identify a resource shortfall
fatigue management
Arrangements to address risks arising from tiredness and work-rest demands.follow fatigue-management requirements
communications plan
The defined channels, contacts, and arrangements for response communication.follow the communications plan
access restriction
A limitation on entry or movement in an affected area.verify access restrictions
request owner
The named person responsible for following up a request.identify the request owner
status reconciliation
Checking that different reports describe the same resources without conflict or duplication.complete status reconciliation

Twelve confirmed lodging spaces are not twenty available spaces. Likewise, crew arrival does not automatically establish briefing, qualification checks, or deployment authorization. The next update time does not promise that the remaining resources will be confirmed.

C Notice the language

Totals can hide different stages

A fast handover often compresses requested, confirmed, and arrived into available. Use the categories explicitly and check whether one count is a subset of another. A concise but structured read-back is more useful than a larger total that double-counts people or resources.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Timestamp the handover
This is the resource position at 19:30 local time.
State the request total
Six external crews were requested.
Separate confirmations
Three crews are confirmed and three remain unconfirmed.
Break down the confirmed group
One confirmed crew is at staging and two are en route.
Avoid double counting
Those three statuses describe the same confirmed group.
Qualify arrival timing
Arrival times for the traveling crews are not verified.
State lodging demand
Twenty lodging spaces were requested.
State lodging confirmation
Twelve spaces are confirmed and eight remain pending.
Name the receiving owner
Sofia has accepted tracking the open requests.
Separate acceptance from supply
Accepting the handover does not confirm the missing resources.
Set the communication time
The next status update is at 20:00 local time.
Avoid an availability claim
Requested does not mean available for deployment.
Preserve operational boundaries
Arrival at staging does not itself authorize a work assignment.
Keep categories consistent
Use requested, confirmed, en route, and arrived as separate status fields.
Report unchanged conditions
The next update will include unresolved requests even if their status is unchanged.
Close with read-back
Please repeat the crew counts, lodging counts, owner, and next update time.
6 language and precision notes
Of the three
Signals a subset rather than an additional count.
En route
Indicates travel, not confirmed arrival.
Pending
Keeps a request open rather than silently treating it as supplied.
Accepted tracking
Transfers follow-up responsibility without creating a supplier commitment.
As of 19:30
Prevents the handover snapshot from appearing timeless.
At 20:00
Commits to an update, not to resolving every resource shortfall.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which crew summary avoids double counting?

Answer and explanation

D. Six requested; three confirmed, comprising one arrived and two en route

The arrived and en-route counts are subsets of the three confirmed crews.

2. Which lodging statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Twelve confirmed; eight pending from a request for twenty

The brief gives twenty requested and twelve confirmed, leaving eight pending.

3. What does the 20:00 commitment promise?

Answer and explanation

C. A new status update

Sofia accepts a communication commitment, not a guarantee of resource delivery or service restoration.

4. What follows from a crew arriving at staging?

Answer and explanation

B. Arrival is established, while other required deployment stages remain separate.

Arrival does not itself establish briefing, qualification verification, or authorization for work.

E Conversations

Requested resources are not all available

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Daniel: Outgoing logistics coordinator / Sofia: Incoming logistics coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • resource shortfall
  • shift handover
  • Lodging allocation
  • staging location
  • estimated time of arrival
  • situation report
  • request owner
  • status reconciliation
  • en route
  • Deployment authorization
  1. Daniel
    This is the resource position at nineteen thirty local time. Six external crews were requested, and three are confirmed. I need to hand over the remaining requests.
  2. Sofia
    I will accept the 1. First, break down those three confirmed crews. I want to distinguish who has arrived from who is still traveling before I update the situation report.
  3. Daniel
    One is at staging and two are en route. Three requested crews remain unconfirmed. Arrival times for the traveling crews are unverified.
  4. Sofia
    So the 2 group is part of the three confirmed, not an additional two. I will not add one arrived and two traveling to the confirmed total again.
  5. Daniel
    Please correct the earlier six-available line. It appears to count the same three crews twice, once as confirmed and again by travel status.
  6. Sofia
    I will correct the 3. It should show six requested, three confirmed, and three unconfirmed, with the arrival breakdown nested within the confirmed group.
  7. Daniel
    Lodging also needs a qualification: twenty spaces requested, twelve confirmed, eight pending. The request remains open.
  8. Sofia
    I will record that 4 explicitly. A request for twenty does not mean twenty spaces are available, and we should not assign accommodation on the basis of the request alone.
  9. Daniel
    Please take ownership of both the unconfirmed crews and the pending lodging spaces. I want the outgoing record to identify who is following them up.
  10. Sofia
    I accept that as the 5. My acceptance transfers coordination responsibility, not supplier confirmation. The open requests remain open until their status is verified and recorded.
  11. Daniel
    When is your next update? The coordination lead needs a consistent time to refresh the resource summary across the response team.
  12. Sofia
    Twenty hundred local time. I will provide a 6 then, including any requests still unresolved. That is an information commitment, not a promise that every crew or lodging space will be confirmed.
  13. Daniel
    The crew at staging has arrived, but this snapshot says nothing about its briefing, qualification checks, or an authorized work assignment. Please keep those stages separate.
  14. Sofia
    Agreed. Arrival at the 7 is not permission to deploy. I will leave the applicable operational checks and assignments with the authorized response process rather than infer them from presence.
  15. Daniel
    Someone may ask whether these crews will restore a neighborhood tonight. Our logistics counts cannot support that promise, and no such assignment is established.
  16. Sofia
    Correct. 8 and restoration planning are separate operational matters. I can report the resource status without claiming when any particular service point will be restored.
  17. Daniel
    The next shift also asked when the two traveling crews will arrive. I have no verified ETA to hand over.
  18. Sofia
    I will mark the 9 as unverified. Any update needs its source and time basis, particularly if access restrictions or other conditions change during the response.
  19. Daniel
    Please read the final numbers back so I can close the administrative handover without leaving a different version in my outgoing report.
  20. Sofia
    Six crews requested, three confirmed: one at staging, two en route. Three unconfirmed. 10 is twelve confirmed, eight pending. I own follow-up and will update at twenty hundred local time.

Answers and explanations
  1. shift handover Turn 2. The shift handover transfers information and responsibility without changing the actual confirmation or arrival status of resources.
  2. en route Turn 4. En route means traveling; these two confirmed crews have not yet arrived at staging.
  3. situation report Turn 6. The situation report must preserve actual categories and counts, correcting the unsupported claim of six available crews.
  4. resource shortfall Turn 8. The resource shortfall is requested minus confirmed capacity, here eight pending lodging spaces.
  5. request owner Turn 10. The request owner accepts follow-up responsibility, which does not itself confirm the missing crews or lodging.
  6. status reconciliation Turn 12. Status reconciliation checks consistent resource reporting and prevents duplicate counting before the next update.
  7. staging location Turn 14. The staging location is an assembly point; arrival does not establish readiness or deployment authorization.
  8. Deployment authorization Turn 16. Deployment authorization permits specified work; the logistics snapshot supplies neither that authorization nor a restoration promise.
  9. estimated time of arrival Turn 18. Estimated time of arrival concerns reaching the destination; confirmed participation does not establish that estimate.
  10. Lodging allocation Turn 20. Lodging allocation must use confirmed capacity: twelve spaces are confirmed, with eight still pending.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Daniel: This is the resource position at nineteen thirty local time. Six external crews were requested, and three are confirmed. I need to hand over the remaining requests.
  2. Sofia: I will accept the shift handover. First, break down those three confirmed crews. I want to distinguish who has arrived from who is still traveling before I update the situation report.
  3. Daniel: One is at staging and two are en route. Three requested crews remain unconfirmed. Arrival times for the traveling crews are unverified.
  4. Sofia: So the en route group is part of the three confirmed, not an additional two. I will not add one arrived and two traveling to the confirmed total again.
  5. Daniel: Please correct the earlier six-available line. It appears to count the same three crews twice, once as confirmed and again by travel status.
  6. Sofia: I will correct the situation report. It should show six requested, three confirmed, and three unconfirmed, with the arrival breakdown nested within the confirmed group.
  7. Daniel: Lodging also needs a qualification: twenty spaces requested, twelve confirmed, eight pending. The request remains open.
  8. Sofia: I will record that resource shortfall explicitly. A request for twenty does not mean twenty spaces are available, and we should not assign accommodation on the basis of the request alone.
  9. Daniel: Please take ownership of both the unconfirmed crews and the pending lodging spaces. I want the outgoing record to identify who is following them up.
  10. Sofia: I accept that as the request owner. My acceptance transfers coordination responsibility, not supplier confirmation. The open requests remain open until their status is verified and recorded.
  11. Daniel: When is your next update? The coordination lead needs a consistent time to refresh the resource summary across the response team.
  12. Sofia: Twenty hundred local time. I will provide a status reconciliation then, including any requests still unresolved. That is an information commitment, not a promise that every crew or lodging space will be confirmed.
  13. Daniel: The crew at staging has arrived, but this snapshot says nothing about its briefing, qualification checks, or an authorized work assignment. Please keep those stages separate.
  14. Sofia: Agreed. Arrival at the staging location is not permission to deploy. I will leave the applicable operational checks and assignments with the authorized response process rather than infer them from presence.
  15. Daniel: Someone may ask whether these crews will restore a neighborhood tonight. Our logistics counts cannot support that promise, and no such assignment is established.
  16. Sofia: Correct. Deployment authorization and restoration planning are separate operational matters. I can report the resource status without claiming when any particular service point will be restored.
  17. Daniel: The next shift also asked when the two traveling crews will arrive. I have no verified ETA to hand over.
  18. Sofia: I will mark the estimated time of arrival as unverified. Any update needs its source and time basis, particularly if access restrictions or other conditions change during the response.
  19. Daniel: Please read the final numbers back so I can close the administrative handover without leaving a different version in my outgoing report.
  20. Sofia: Six crews requested, three confirmed: one at staging, two en route. Three unconfirmed. Lodging allocation is twelve confirmed, eight pending. I own follow-up and will update at twenty hundred local time.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Requested crews are not confirmed crews

Fictional storm-response coordination handoff: four mutual-aid crews were requested, two confirmed, and staging details remain open; no field deployment instructions are supplied.

  1. Outgoing response coordinator: Four mutual-aid crews were requested, but only two are confirmed in the current response record.
  2. Incoming response coordinator: I'll take resource follow-up. Are staging arrangements confirmed for those two accepted crews?
  3. Outgoing response coordinator: Not yet. The record still shows crew staging details pending with the responsible coordination team.
  4. Incoming response coordinator: Then I shouldn't brief management as four crews ready for deployment at the staging location.
  5. Outgoing response coordinator: Correct. Request, confirmation, and staging readiness are separate statuses in this handoff.
  6. Incoming response coordinator: I'll contact the listed coordination team and maintain the distinction in the resource summary.
  7. Outgoing response coordinator: Please leave restoration-priority decisions with the authorized emergency-response process, not assumptions from crew counts.
  8. Incoming response coordinator: Understood. This handoff transfers resource follow-up, not authority to decide field deployment or restoration priorities.
  9. Outgoing response coordinator: Can you confirm the quantities and open item before I finish the shift?
  10. Incoming response coordinator: Four requested, two confirmed, staging pending; I accept the next coordination contact and status update.
Lodging accepted, transport not arranged

Separate fictional storm-logistics case: lodging for ten support staff is confirmed, but transport from the arrival point is only requested.

  1. Storm logistics planner: Lodging for ten support staff is confirmed. Their transport request is still awaiting acceptance.
  2. Incoming logistics lead: I accept transport follow-up. Has anyone told the staff that pickup is already arranged?
  3. Storm logistics planner: The draft says all logistics complete, but it hasn't been sent to them yet.
  4. Incoming logistics lead: I'll correct that before release. Lodging confirmation shouldn't hide an unresolved transport arrangement.
  5. Storm logistics planner: The request reference and receiving contact are linked in the emergency-response coordination record.
  6. Incoming logistics lead: I'll check with that contact and ask who has accepted responsibility for the transport task.
  7. Storm logistics planner: Please don't invent a pickup time while waiting; none is confirmed in our current information.
  8. Incoming logistics lead: Agreed. I'll tell staff what is confirmed and who is checking the remaining arrangement.
  9. Storm logistics planner: So lodging stays confirmed, transport stays requested, and your shift owns the next contact?
  10. Incoming logistics lead: Yes. I'll update the record with verified acceptance or keep the unresolved status explicit.
A resource cancellation needs a receiving owner

Separate fictional coordination case: one previously confirmed support vehicle is withdrawn; a replacement is requested but unconfirmed during shift change.

  1. Outgoing resource officer: One confirmed support vehicle has been withdrawn. A replacement is requested, but not yet accepted.
  2. Incoming resource officer: I'll take replacement follow-up. Does the current emergency-response summary still count the withdrawn vehicle?
  3. Outgoing resource officer: Yes, the summary hasn't been revised since the withdrawal notice arrived during this handoff.
  4. Incoming resource officer: I'll correct that count and keep the replacement request separate from confirmed resources.
  5. Outgoing resource officer: Please preserve the withdrawal notice so the next coordinator can understand the change in availability.
  6. Incoming resource officer: I will. Has an authorized team assessed the effect on the current coordination plan?
  7. Outgoing resource officer: That assessment is still pending. We shouldn't invent a deployment change ourselves from this resource update.
  8. Incoming resource officer: Then I'll request the assessment and identify its status alongside the replacement request.
  9. Outgoing resource officer: Thank you. Please confirm you own both contacts, without calling the vehicle shortage resolved.
  10. Incoming resource officer: Confirmed. I'll coordinate replacement status and the authorized impact review, with both open until verified.

Count subsets only once

Eight crews are requested; five confirmed crews comprise two at staging and three en route. Three remain unconfirmed. Eighteen lodging spaces are requested and fourteen confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The confirmed crew total is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. five

Two at staging plus three en route make five confirmed crews.

2. Receiver: "The unconfirmed crew count is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. three

Eight requested minus five confirmed leaves three unconfirmed crews.

3. Coordinator: "Confirmed lodging spaces total ___."

Answer and explanation

A. fourteen

Fourteen is the supplied count of confirmed accommodation spaces.

4. Receiver: "The lodging shortfall is ___ spaces."

Answer and explanation

A. four

Eighteen requested minus fourteen confirmed leaves four spaces pending.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Emergency Preparedness and Storm Response
Original fictional case: At the 19:30 local coordination handover, six external crews have been requested. Three are confirmed: one is at the staging location and two are en route. The other three remain unconfirmed. Twenty lodging spaces were requested; twelve are confirmed and eight are pending. Arrival times for the two traveling crews are not verified. Outgoing coordinator Daniel hands over to Sofia, who accepts tracking the open requests and providing the next status update at 20:00. Confirmed, arrived, briefed, and authorized for deployment are separate stages; this record establishes no new operational assignment or restoration promise.
Language workshop: Requested resources are not all available
Communication goal: Hand over storm-response resources with exact counts, distinct statuses, and a clear receiving owner.
Language guidance: The one arrived crew and two en-route crews are subsets of the three confirmed crews. They are not three additional crews. Six requested minus three confirmed leaves three requests unconfirmed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is the resource position at 19:30 local time. / Six external crews were requested. / Three crews are confirmed and three remain unconfirmed. / One confirmed crew is at staging and two are en route. / Those three statuses describe the same confirmed group. / Arrival times for the traveling crews are not verified. / Twenty lodging spaces were requested. / Twelve spaces are confirmed and eight remain pending. / Sofia has accepted tracking the open requests. / Accepting the handover does not confirm the missing resources. / The next status update is at 20:00 local time. / Requested does not mean available for deployment. / Arrival at staging does not itself authorize a work assignment. / Use requested, confirmed, en route, and arrived as separate status fields. / The next update will include unresolved requests even if their status is unchanged. / Please repeat the crew counts, lodging counts, owner, and next update time.
Vocabulary: emergency operations center (EOC): A location or function coordinating information and resources during an emergency. / incident command: The authority structure directing an incident response under the applicable system. / mutual assistance: Support provided by other organizations under applicable arrangements. / resource request: A documented request for specified people, equipment, or support. / resource confirmation: Verification that the requested resource is committed on stated terms. / staging location: A designated place where resources assemble before further assignment. / mobilization: Preparing and moving resources for an assigned response. / demobilization: Organized release and return of resources when no longer required. / en route: Traveling toward the relevant destination, not yet arrived. / estimated time of arrival (ETA): An estimate of when a person or resource will reach a destination. / deployment authorization: Permission to assign or send resources to specified work under the applicable process. / crew roster: A list of personnel assigned to a crew or response. / credential verification: Checking the relevant qualifications or authorizations of personnel. / shift handover: Transfer of current information and responsibilities between shifts. / operational period: A defined interval used for response planning and coordination. / situation report: A structured update of current conditions, actions, and resource status. / logistics: Coordination of resources, movement, accommodation, and other support. / lodging allocation: Assignment of accommodation capacity to the relevant personnel. / resource shortfall: The gap between required or requested resources and those available or confirmed. / fatigue management: Arrangements to address risks arising from tiredness and work-rest demands. / communications plan: The defined channels, contacts, and arrangements for response communication. / access restriction: A limitation on entry or movement in an affected area. / request owner: The named person responsible for following up a request. / status reconciliation: Checking that different reports describe the same resources without conflict or duplication.

Read the checked transfer twice using five confirmed crews, three unconfirmed, 14 lodging spaces, and four still needed.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 06: Emergency Preparedness and Storm Response
Original fictional case: At the 19:30 local coordination handover, six external crews have been requested. Three are confirmed: one is at the staging location and two are en route. The other three remain unconfirmed. Twenty lodging spaces were requested; twelve are confirmed and eight are pending. Arrival times for the two traveling crews are not verified. Outgoing coordinator Daniel hands over to Sofia, who accepts tracking the open requests and providing the next status update at 20:00. Confirmed, arrived, briefed, and authorized for deployment are separate stages; this record establishes no new operational assignment or restoration promise.
Language workshop: Requested resources are not all available
Communication goal: Hand over storm-response resources with exact counts, distinct statuses, and a clear receiving owner.
Language guidance: The one arrived crew and two en-route crews are subsets of the three confirmed crews. They are not three additional crews. Six requested minus three confirmed leaves three requests unconfirmed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is the resource position at 19:30 local time. / Six external crews were requested. / Three crews are confirmed and three remain unconfirmed. / One confirmed crew is at staging and two are en route. / Those three statuses describe the same confirmed group. / Arrival times for the traveling crews are not verified. / Twenty lodging spaces were requested. / Twelve spaces are confirmed and eight remain pending. / Sofia has accepted tracking the open requests. / Accepting the handover does not confirm the missing resources. / The next status update is at 20:00 local time. / Requested does not mean available for deployment. / Arrival at staging does not itself authorize a work assignment. / Use requested, confirmed, en route, and arrived as separate status fields. / The next update will include unresolved requests even if their status is unchanged. / Please repeat the crew counts, lodging counts, owner, and next update time.
Vocabulary: emergency operations center (EOC): A location or function coordinating information and resources during an emergency. / incident command: The authority structure directing an incident response under the applicable system. / mutual assistance: Support provided by other organizations under applicable arrangements. / resource request: A documented request for specified people, equipment, or support. / resource confirmation: Verification that the requested resource is committed on stated terms. / staging location: A designated place where resources assemble before further assignment. / mobilization: Preparing and moving resources for an assigned response. / demobilization: Organized release and return of resources when no longer required. / en route: Traveling toward the relevant destination, not yet arrived. / estimated time of arrival (ETA): An estimate of when a person or resource will reach a destination. / deployment authorization: Permission to assign or send resources to specified work under the applicable process. / crew roster: A list of personnel assigned to a crew or response. / credential verification: Checking the relevant qualifications or authorizations of personnel. / shift handover: Transfer of current information and responsibilities between shifts. / operational period: A defined interval used for response planning and coordination. / situation report: A structured update of current conditions, actions, and resource status. / logistics: Coordination of resources, movement, accommodation, and other support. / lodging allocation: Assignment of accommodation capacity to the relevant personnel. / resource shortfall: The gap between required or requested resources and those available or confirmed. / fatigue management: Arrangements to address risks arising from tiredness and work-rest demands. / communications plan: The defined channels, contacts, and arrangements for response communication. / access restriction: A limitation on entry or movement in an affected area. / request owner: The named person responsible for following up a request. / status reconciliation: Checking that different reports describe the same resources without conflict or duplication.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue with one arrived plus two en route within the three confirmed crews. Switch roles; distinguish 12 confirmed lodging spaces from 20 requested and 20:00 from an arrival promise. Read the checked transfer twice using five confirmed crews, three unconfirmed, 14 lodging spaces, and four still needed.
Partner: Daniel: Outgoing logistics coordinator / Sofia: Incoming logistics coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Eight crews are requested; five confirmed crews comprise two at staging and three en route. Three remain unconfirmed. Eighteen lodging spaces are requested and fourteen confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Daniel: The next shift also asked when the two traveling crews will arrive. I have no verified ETA to hand over.
Sofia: I will mark the estimated time of arrival as unverified. Any update needs its source and time basis, particularly if access restrictions or other conditions change during the response.
Daniel: Please read the final numbers back so I can close the administrative handover without leaving a different version in my outgoing report.
Sofia: Six crews requested, three confirmed: one at staging, two en route. Three unconfirmed. Lodging allocation is twelve confirmed, eight pending. I own follow-up and will update at twenty hundred local time.
END REFERENCE
07Customer Programs and Energy EfficiencyLower consumption does not isolate the program effect+

Your goal

Report an observed energy reduction while separating measurement, explanation, and verified savings.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Lower consumption does not isolate the program effect

A fictional office used 10,000 kWh in a baseline month and 8,500 kWh in a post-installation month after an efficiency retrofit. Both readings cover 30 days. The second month had milder weather and average daily occupancy fell from 40 to 30 people. No adjusted baseline or agreed savings-verification analysis has been completed. Program specialist Leila finds a draft claiming the retrofit proved 15% savings and a 15% bill reduction. Analyst Victor must help report the observed 1,500 kWh, or 15%, consumption decline without assigning the entire change to the retrofit. Rates and bill components are not supplied.

Leila: Efficiency program specialist / Victor: Energy analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What measured energy change is supplied?

Answer and explanation

C. A 1,500 kWh reduction, equal to 15% of baseline use

Ten thousand minus 8,500 is 1,500 kWh, which is 15% of the baseline 10,000.

2. Which other conditions changed?

Answer and explanation

B. Weather and occupancy

The brief states milder weather and a fall in average daily occupancy from forty to thirty.

3. What has not been completed?

Answer and explanation

D. An adjusted baseline and savings-verification analysis

The observations exist, but the analysis needed to isolate a savings effect remains incomplete.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The observed reduction is 1,500 kWh divided by the baseline 10,000 kWh, or 15%. It is measured consumption change, not yet a verified retrofit effect. Milder weather and lower occupancy also changed the comparison.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
energy efficiency
Providing the relevant service with less energy under a defined comparison.improve energy efficiency
energy conservation
Reducing energy use through changes in behavior, service, or activity.encourage energy conservation
retrofit
A modification or addition to improve an existing system or building.assess an efficiency retrofit
energy conservation measure (ECM)
A defined intervention intended to reduce energy or related resource use.evaluate an energy conservation measure
baseline period
The reference period used to establish pre-intervention conditions or use.define the baseline period
reporting period
The period for which performance or results are being assessed.identify the reporting period
metered consumption
Energy use measured by the relevant meter over a stated interval.compare metered consumption
measurement and verification (M&V)
A defined process for assessing and verifying savings against an appropriate basis.develop a measurement and verification plan
adjusted baseline
Reference use adjusted to relevant comparison conditions under an explicit method.calculate an adjusted baseline
weather normalization
Adjusting energy analysis to a defined weather basis.apply weather normalization
heating degree days
A weather indicator based on temperature relative to a specified heating reference.use heating degree days
cooling degree days
A weather indicator based on temperature relative to a specified cooling reference.use cooling degree days
occupancy
The presence or number of people using a space over a defined basis.track occupancy changes
operating hours
The time during which a facility or system is operated.document operating hours
independent variable
An input used to explain variation in another measured quantity.identify relevant independent variables
confounding factor
A factor that complicates attribution of an observed change to one cause.account for confounding factors
attribution
Assigning an observed effect to a particular cause or intervention.qualify savings attribution
avoided energy use
Energy estimated not to have been used compared with an appropriate counterfactual basis.estimate avoided energy use
gross savings
Savings associated with an intervention before specified program-attribution adjustments.distinguish gross savings
net savings
Program savings after the relevant adjustments for attribution and other specified effects.assess net savings
rebound effect
Increased use of a service that may offset some expected efficiency savings.assess the rebound effect
persistence
The extent to which an effect continues over time.evaluate savings persistence
uncertainty
The limits on confidence or precision in an estimate or conclusion.report savings uncertainty
bill savings
A reduction in monetary charges under the relevant rates and billing conditions.distinguish bill savings

Energy savings and bill savings are not interchangeable. Prices, fixed charges, demand charges, and other billing components can affect the money paid. This case supplies no rate information, so it supports no bill-savings percentage.

C Notice the language

A qualified result can still be useful

Teams may feel pressure to turn a promising result into a simple success claim. State the observed change clearly, then name the factors that prevent stronger attribution. This preserves useful evidence without treating either enthusiasm or skepticism as a substitute for analysis.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the readings
Consumption fell from ten thousand to eight thousand five hundred kilowatt-hours.
Name the period
Both readings cover thirty days.
Calculate the difference
The observed reduction is fifteen hundred kilowatt-hours.
State the denominator
That is fifteen percent of baseline consumption.
Qualify attribution
We cannot assign the whole decline to the retrofit from these readings alone.
Name the weather change
The reporting month had milder weather.
Name the occupancy change
Average daily occupancy fell from forty to thirty people.
Avoid a false constant
The comparison conditions were not unchanged.
Identify the missing analysis
An adjusted baseline and verification analysis have not been completed.
Distinguish the measures
Lower energy use does not automatically mean the same percentage reduction in the bill.
Preserve the unit
These figures are energy in kilowatt-hours, not peak power in kilowatts.
Correct the claim
Replace proved savings with observed consumption reduction.
Keep the result useful
The decline is real in the supplied readings, while its causes remain to be assessed.
State the method boundary
Use the applicable measurement and verification plan for a verified savings claim.
Avoid invented adjustments
Do not subtract an assumed weather effect without a supported method.
Close with the evidence
Report the readings, changed conditions, and unresolved attribution together.
6 language and precision notes
Fell from
Describes an observed change without assigning its cause.
Because of
Adds a causal claim that these readings alone do not establish.
Of baseline
Identifies the denominator for the 15% calculation.
Same period length
Removes one difference but does not make all conditions comparable.
Not yet verified
Does not mean that the intervention had no effect.
Energy versus money
Distinguishes consumption units from the monetary bill.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which headline is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Metered use fell 15%; retrofit attribution remains unverified.

The readings support a consumption decline but not the causal, billing, or peak-demand claims.

2. Why do equal 30-day periods not settle attribution?

Answer and explanation

D. Weather and occupancy still differed.

Equal duration controls one comparison feature but does not remove the stated weather and occupancy differences.

3. What is the occupancy decline?

Answer and explanation

B. 25%, from 40 to 30 people

Ten fewer divided by the original forty equals twenty-five percent.

4. Which bill statement is justified?

Answer and explanation

C. Bill savings cannot be calculated from the supplied information.

Rates and billing components are missing, so consumption figures alone do not determine monetary savings.

E Conversations

Lower consumption does not isolate the program effect

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leila: Efficiency program specialist / Victor: Energy analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • adjusted baseline
  • bill savings
  • Occupancy
  • Uncertainty
  • reporting period
  • attribution
  • measurement and verification
  • confounding factors
  • metered consumption
  • Weather normalization
  1. Leila
    The draft says our retrofit proved fifteen percent energy savings and the same reduction in bills. The meter readings fell, but can we support both claims?
  2. Victor
    We can support the 1 change: ten thousand to eight thousand five hundred kilowatt-hours. That is fifteen hundred less, or fifteen percent of the original reading.
  3. Leila
    Both readings cover thirty days. I initially thought matching the period length meant we could attribute the whole difference to the equipment installed between them.
  4. Victor
    It removes one difference, but the 2 had milder weather and fewer occupants. Those changes can affect energy use, so the conditions were not otherwise held constant.
  5. Leila
    Average daily occupancy fell from forty to thirty people. That is ten fewer, or twenty-five percent of the original forty, not a ten percent change.
  6. Victor
    Correct. 3 is a separate measured condition. We cannot assume its effect on energy is proportional to the headcount change or simply subtract twenty-five percent.
  7. Leila
    So we should not try to correct the energy result with a guessed number for people, then assign whatever remains to the retrofit.
  8. Victor
    Exactly. An 4 needs an explicit, supported method. It is not an informal subtraction that makes the result fit the program story.
  9. Leila
    The weather changed too. The second month was milder, but we have not quantified the resulting heating or cooling effect in this analysis.
  10. Victor
    5 may form part of the applicable method. The appropriate approach must be established from the project and data, not improvised from the word milder.
  11. Leila
    I do not want to discard the meter result. Can the headline state the fifteen-percent consumption decline and flag the attribution separately?
  12. Victor
    Say observed consumption fell fifteen percent, with 6 to the retrofit unverified. That preserves both the measured result and the limit on what it demonstrates about the intervention.
  13. Leila
    The program manager heard unverified as no benefit. Please help me correct that impression without claiming an effect we have not measured.
  14. Victor
    No. 7 is not a finding of zero benefit. We have not isolated the effect, and that means we should avoid both an exact success claim and an unsupported dismissal.
  15. Leila
    The monetary claim needs correcting as well. We have no rates, fixed charges, credits, demand charges, or other billing information in the supplied case.
  16. Victor
    Then we cannot calculate 8. A fifteen percent energy decline does not automatically produce a fifteen percent bill decline, even when the energy readings themselves are accurate.
  17. Leila
    I will keep the units visible. Kilowatt-hours measure the energy used over the period; these monthly readings do not establish what happened to peak demand.
  18. Victor
    Good. The 9 process needs the relevant comparison conditions and method. Our immediate communication should identify the missing analysis rather than invent its outcome.
  19. Leila
    The revised summary will give both readings, the equal thirty-day periods, the weather and occupancy changes, and the fact that no adjusted analysis is complete.
  20. Victor
    That makes the 10 visible. Keep the fifteen percent as an observed consumption reduction, not a certified savings result or a promise about future bills.

Answers and explanations
  1. metered consumption Turn 2. Metered consumption fell by 1,500 kWh; those observations alone do not establish the cause.
  2. reporting period Turn 4. The reporting period had different weather and occupancy; equal duration does not make all conditions comparable.
  3. Occupancy Turn 6. Occupancy fell from forty to thirty people; its contribution to the energy decline requires analysis.
  4. adjusted baseline Turn 8. An adjusted baseline represents reference use under relevant comparison conditions using an explicit method, not arbitrary subtraction.
  5. Weather normalization Turn 10. Weather normalization adjusts analysis to a defined weather basis; it has not been completed here.
  6. attribution Turn 12. Attribution concerns how much change is due to the retrofit; that causal question remains unresolved.
  7. Uncertainty Turn 14. Uncertainty limits the supported conclusion; it is not proof that the retrofit had no benefit.
  8. bill savings Turn 16. Bill savings concerns money under actual billing conditions; missing rates and charges prevent a supported percentage.
  9. measurement and verification Turn 18. Measurement and verification supplies the defined basis for assessing savings and must address relevant comparison conditions.
  10. confounding factors Turn 20. Confounding factors complicate attribution, here weather and occupancy changes occurring alongside the retrofit.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leila: The draft says our retrofit proved fifteen percent energy savings and the same reduction in bills. The meter readings fell, but can we support both claims?

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Lower energy use has several possible explanations

Fictional program evaluation: measured energy use fell after installation, while weather and occupancy also changed; attribution has not been established.

  1. Efficiency program manager: Energy use fell after installation, so the program caused the entire reduction in this building.
  2. Evaluation analyst: The reduction is observed, but weather and occupancy changed too. Attribution remains unresolved.
  3. Efficiency program manager: Could you explain why the before-and-after baseline comparison doesn't settle the question by itself?
  4. Evaluation analyst: It compares different conditions. Measurement and verification needs to address those differences before attributing savings.
  5. Efficiency program manager: Then we can report lower measured use, but not claim every saved unit came from installation.
  6. Evaluation analyst: Exactly. Keep the observation and the causal interpretation separate in the customer-program update.
  7. Efficiency program manager: Should we promise the same reduction to every applicant considering the incentive this month?
  8. Evaluation analyst: No. This incomplete single-building comparison doesn't establish results for other participants or operating conditions.
  9. Efficiency program manager: I'll request the adjusted analysis and label the current result as an observed change only.
  10. Evaluation analyst: Good. That keeps the program message useful without overstating verified savings or individual outcomes.
Enrollment is not demonstrated demand reduction

Separate fictional demand-response pilot: forty businesses enrolled; event participation and measured reductions have not yet been reviewed.

  1. Customer program lead: Forty businesses enrolled in the demand-response pilot. Can we report forty successful reductions?
  2. Measurement specialist: Enrollment is confirmed, but event participation and measured demand changes haven't been reviewed yet.
  3. Customer program lead: I thought signing up showed that each business delivered the expected response during the event.
  4. Measurement specialist: It shows agreement to participate, not what happened during a specific measured event.
  5. Customer program lead: What evidence would the evaluation team need before making a performance statement about those businesses?
  6. Measurement specialist: They need the relevant event data and the agreed baseline method, reviewed for this pilot.
  7. Customer program lead: Could we call the incentive cost money saved while that analysis is still pending?
  8. Measurement specialist: Not from enrollment alone. Program cost and verified savings are different figures with separate evidence.
  9. Customer program lead: I'll celebrate the enrollment milestone while clearly stating that performance verification remains open.
  10. Measurement specialist: That recognizes participation without converting a program-registration count into a measured energy outcome.
A projected saving is not a meter result

Separate fictional efficiency case: a proposal models a 10% saving, but installation has not occurred and no post-installation measurements exist.

  1. Program adviser: The proposal shows ten percent savings. Can the customer newsletter say we achieved that reduction?
  2. Verification coordinator: No. It's a modeled projection before installation, not a result from measured energy use.
  3. Program adviser: Then should we remove it entirely because it isn't yet a verified outcome?
  4. Verification coordinator: You can retain it as a clearly labeled estimate, with the relevant assumptions and scope.
  5. Program adviser: The model uses the proposed operating schedule. That schedule hasn't been confirmed by the customer.
  6. Verification coordinator: Then identify that assumption too. An unconfirmed schedule can affect the interpretation of the projection.
  7. Program adviser: I'll say projected under the model assumptions, not guaranteed savings for this customer's bills.
  8. Verification coordinator: Good. Any later measurement and verification should use the agreed method and actual relevant data.
  9. Program adviser: I'll ask the customer-program team to confirm the schedule before the proposal is finalized.
  10. Verification coordinator: That keeps projected performance, installation status, and measured results distinct throughout the conversation.

Answer and explanation

particular cause or intervention. / avoided energy use: Energy estimated not to have been used compared with an appropriate counterfactual basis. / gross savings: Savings associated with an intervention before specified program-attribution adjustments. / net savings: Program savings after the relevant adjustments for attribution and other specified effects. / rebound effect: Increased use of a service that may offset some expected efficiency savings. / persistence: The extent to which an effect continues over time. / uncertainty: The limits on confidence or precision in an estimate or conclusion. / bill savings: A reduction in monetary charges under the relevant rates and billing conditions.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue with 1,500 kWh and 15 percent as the observed consumption decline. Switch roles; distinguish the 25 percent occupancy decline from an unmeasured energy effect. Read the checked transfer twice with 1,200 kWh and 10 percent, preserving unverified attribution and unavailable bill savings.
Partner: Leila: Efficiency program specialist / Victor: Energy analyst

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 07: Customer Programs and Energy Efficiency
Original fictional case: A fictional office used 10,000 kWh in a baseline month and 8,500 kWh in a post-installation month after an efficiency retrofit. Both readings cover 30 days. The second month had milder weather and average daily occupancy fell from 40 to 30 people. No adjusted baseline or agreed savings-verification analysis has been completed. Program specialist Leila finds a draft claiming the retrofit proved 15% savings and a 15% bill reduction. Analyst Victor must help report the observed 1,500 kWh, or 15%, consumption decline without assigning the entire change to the retrofit. Rates and bill components are not supplied.
Language workshop: Lower consumption does not isolate the program effect
Communication goal: Report an observed energy reduction while separating measurement, explanation, and verified savings.
Language guidance: The observed reduction is 1,500 kWh divided by the baseline 10,000 kWh, or 15%. It is measured consumption change, not yet a verified retrofit effect. Milder weather and lower occupancy also changed the comparison.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Consumption fell from ten thousand to eight thousand five hundred kilowatt-hours. / Both readings cover thirty days. / The observed reduction is fifteen hundred kilowatt-hours. / That is fifteen percent of baseline consumption. / We cannot assign the whole decline to the retrofit from these readings alone. / The reporting month had milder weather. / Average daily occupancy fell from forty to thirty people. / The comparison conditions were not unchanged. / An adjusted baseline and verification analysis have not been completed. / Lower energy use does not automatically mean the same percentage reduction in the bill. / These figures are energy in kilowatt-hours, not peak power in kilowatts. / Replace proved savings with observed consumption reduction. / The decline is real in the supplied readings, while its causes remain to be assessed. / Use the applicable measurement and verification plan for a verified savings claim. / Do not subtract an assumed weather effect without a supported method. / Report the readings, changed conditions, and unresolved attribution together.
Vocabulary: energy efficiency: Providing the relevant service with less energy under a defined comparison. / energy conservation: Reducing energy use through changes in behavior, service, or activity. / retrofit: A modification or addition to improve an existing system or building. / energy conservation measure (ECM): A defined intervention intended to reduce energy or related resource use. / baseline period: The reference period used to establish pre-intervention conditions or use. / reporting period: The period for which performance or results are being assessed. / metered consumption: Energy use measured by the relevant meter over a stated interval. / measurement and verification (M&V): A defined process for assessing and verifying savings against an appropriate basis. / adjusted baseline: Reference use adjusted to relevant comparison conditions under an explicit method. / weather normalization: Adjusting energy analysis to a defined weather basis. / heating degree days: A weather indicator based on temperature relative to a specified heating reference. / cooling degree days: A weather indicator based on temperature relative to a specified cooling reference. / occupancy: The presence or number of people using a space over a defined basis. / operating hours: The time during which a facility or system is operated. / independent variable: An input used to TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Executive Reliability and Investment UpdatesFewer interruptions, more total outage minutes+

Your goal

Translate reliability indices, check a comparison, and distinguish a metric trend from an investment promise.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Fewer interruptions, more total outage minutes

A fictional utility serves 1,000 customers in each of two years. Using the same sustained-interruption definitions and event coverage, Year 1 records 1,200 customer interruptions and 90,000 customer-minutes interrupted. Year 2 records 1,000 customer interruptions and 120,000 customer-minutes interrupted. The same major-event treatment applies in both years. Analyst Ravi presents the results to community liaison Celia. A draft headline says reliability improved on every measure. A proposed investment is also described as guaranteed to reduce next-year interruptions, although no validated impact estimate is supplied. Both claims need qualification.

Ravi: Reliability analyst / Celia: Community liaison

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Year 1 SAIFI?

Answer and explanation

C. 1.2 interruptions per customer

Divide 1,200 customer interruptions by 1,000 customers to obtain 1.2.

2. What is Year 2 SAIDI?

Answer and explanation

D. 120 minutes per customer

Divide 120,000 customer-minutes by 1,000 customers to obtain 120 minutes.

3. Which overall description matches the two indices?

Answer and explanation

D. Average frequency fell while average total interruption duration rose.

SAIFI falls from 1.2 to 1.0, while SAIDI rises from 90 to 120 minutes.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

SAIFI is 1.2 in Year 1 and 1.0 in Year 2. SAIDI is 90 and 120 minutes respectively. Frequency fell, but total interruption duration per customer rose. The headline improved on every measure is therefore false.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
System Average Interruption Duration Index (SAIDI)
Average total sustained-interruption time per customer over a stated period.explain SAIDI in minutes
System Average Interruption Frequency Index (SAIFI)
Average sustained-interruption count per customer over a stated period.explain SAIFI as frequency
sustained interruption
An interruption meeting the duration threshold in the applicable reporting definition.define sustained interruptions
customer interruption
One counted interruption affecting one customer.count customer interruptions
customer-minute interrupted
One interruption minute for one customer.sum customer-minutes interrupted
customers served
The customer population used as the denominator.state customers served
Customer Average Interruption Duration Index (CAIDI)
Average duration per sustained customer interruption on a stated basis.distinguish CAIDI from SAIDI
momentary interruption
A short interruption classified under the applicable reporting definition.distinguish momentary interruptions
major event day
A day classified as a major event under the stated reliability methodology.state major-event-day treatment
event coverage
The set of events included in a reported measure.align event coverage
reporting methodology
The definitions and procedures used to calculate the measures.document the reporting methodology
denominator
The quantity by which another is divided to calculate a ratio or average.check the denominator
frequency
How often counted events occur over the stated period or basis.explain interruption frequency
duration
How long an interruption or accumulated interruption time lasts.explain interruption duration
system average
A measure averaged over the defined system population.qualify the system average
distribution of outcomes
How results vary across customers, locations, or events.examine the distribution of outcomes
reliability trend
The direction of change in defined service-reliability measures over time.interpret the reliability trend
resilience
The ability to withstand, adapt to, and recover from disruptive events.distinguish resilience from a single reliability index
investment proposal
A plan requesting resources for a specified project or improvement.assess an investment proposal
benefit estimate
An estimate of the favorable effects expected from an action.validate the benefit estimate
business case
The stated rationale, costs, benefits, risks, and assumptions supporting a decision.review the business case
counterfactual
An estimate or model of what would occur without the intervention.establish the counterfactual basis
target outcome
The result an initiative aims to achieve, not a guaranteed result.distinguish the target outcome
performance commitment
A defined undertaking about performance on a specified basis.qualify the performance commitment

On this same basis, CAIDI is 90/1.2 = 75 minutes in Year 1 and 120/1.0 = 120 minutes in Year 2. These averages do not mean every customer had identical experiences, and historical trends do not guarantee an investment effect.

C Notice the language

Translate an average without turning it into everybody

Community members may compare an average with their own outage experience and conclude that one must be wrong. Explain the population and units. An average can describe the system accurately while concealing substantial differences between individual customers or neighborhoods.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Define frequency
SAIFI describes average sustained interruptions per customer.
Define total duration
SAIDI describes average total sustained-interruption minutes per customer.
State the period
These figures cover each full reporting year.
Show the denominator
Both years use one thousand customers.
Calculate Year 1 frequency
Twelve hundred divided by one thousand gives 1.2.
Calculate Year 2 frequency
One thousand divided by one thousand gives 1.0.
Calculate Year 1 duration
Ninety thousand customer-minutes divided by one thousand gives ninety minutes.
Calculate Year 2 duration
One hundred twenty thousand divided by one thousand gives one hundred twenty minutes.
Summarize the mixed trend
Interruptions were less frequent on average, but total interruption time increased.
Correct the headline
The data do not show improvement on every measure.
Qualify the average
Individual customers may have experienced different outcomes.
Check the comparison
Use the same definitions, event coverage, and major-event treatment.
Distinguish CAIDI
CAIDI describes average duration per customer interruption, not per customer served.
Separate resilience
A single annual index does not describe every aspect of resilience.
Qualify the investment
The proposed benefit needs a supported estimate and assumptions.
Close with the decision
Present the measured trend separately from the investment proposal and its uncertainty.
6 language and precision notes
Per customer
Uses the served population, including those with no counted interruption.
Per interruption
Uses interruption counts rather than all customers served.
Total duration
Accumulates time across relevant interruptions.
On average
Does not claim that every customer experienced the same result.
Same coverage
Makes the comparison meaningful across reporting periods.
Guaranteed
Is unsupported by the unvalidated investment estimate in this case.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which Year 1 calculation gives CAIDI?

Answer and explanation

C. 90,000 customer-minutes divided by 1,200 customer interruptions = 75 minutes

CAIDI divides accumulated customer-interruption minutes by customer interruptions, giving seventy-five minutes.

2. Which interpretation of Year 2 SAIFI is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. The system average is one counted interruption per customer.

A system average does not require identical interruption counts or experiences for every customer.

3. What must be aligned for the comparison?

Answer and explanation

C. Definitions, reporting periods, population basis, and event coverage

Changing measurement scope can create a misleading trend even when each separate calculation is correct.

4. Which investment statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. The benefit remains a proposal requiring a validated basis.

No validated impact estimate is supplied, so the promised improvement is not established.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Fewer interruptions, more total outage minutes

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ravi: Reliability analyst / Celia: Community liaison

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  • reporting methodology

  • CAIDI
  • target outcome

  • resilience

  1. Celia
    The headline says reliability improved on every measure, but the chart shows SAIDI increasing. I need a clear explanation before presenting this to community representatives.
  2. Ravi
    Start with 1. SAIFI is the average count of sustained interruptions per customer. It falls from one point two in Year 1 to one in Year 2.
  3. Celia
    We get those figures by dividing twelve hundred customer interruptions and then one thousand by the same thousand customers served in each year.
  4. Ravi
    Correct. Keep the 2 visible. It is the defined customer population, not only the customers who experienced interruptions, and it does not count network equipment failures.
  5. Celia
    Residents will challenge an average of one if they personally had three interruptions. I want to explain that without dismissing their experience.
  6. Ravi
    Exactly. A 3 can coexist with uneven outcomes. Some customers may have none and others several; the average alone does not describe that distribution.
  7. Celia
    Now the duration measure. We have ninety thousand customer-minutes in Year 1 and a hundred twenty thousand in Year 2, again across a thousand customers.
  8. Ravi
    That gives 4 of ninety and one hundred twenty minutes. It measures total interruption time per customer over the year, not the length of one typical incident.
  9. Celia
    Then interruptions became less frequent on average, but customers experienced more total interruption time on the system-average basis. That is a mixed trend, not universal improvement.
  10. Ravi
    Yes. The 5 measure worsened while frequency improved. We must say both, rather than choose whichever number supports the preferred headline about overall progress.
  11. Celia
    Someone may ask how long an interruption lasted on average. That requires a different denominator from the number of customers served, does it not?
  12. Ravi
    Use 6 on this same measurement basis: ninety thousand divided by twelve hundred is seventy-five minutes; one hundred twenty thousand divided by one thousand is one hundred twenty minutes.
  13. Celia
    So CAIDI is duration per customer interruption, while SAIDI is accumulated duration per customer served. Similar acronyms conceal an important distinction.
  14. Ravi
    Correct. Also preserve the 7. The supplied years use the same sustained-interruption definitions and major-event treatment, which is necessary for interpreting the comparison we just made.
  15. Celia
    Please put the major-event treatment next to the chart. If that changes between years, we need to explain it before comparing the trend.
  16. Ravi
    Exactly. State the 8, period, and scope. Readers should not have to discover a changed definition after reacting to a large improvement or deterioration in the headline.
  17. Celia
    The investment slide promises fewer interruptions next year, but we have no validated estimate explaining the size or certainty of that effect.
  18. Ravi
    Then label it a 9, not a guarantee. The business case needs a supported benefit estimate, assumptions, costs, and risks; these historical indices cannot supply that causal forecast by themselves.
  19. Celia
    I will present the two measured trends, explain the averages, and place the investment proposal in a separate section with its remaining evidence needs.
  20. Ravi
    Good. Community questions about 10 may reach beyond these annual averages. Explain what the indices measure accurately, then identify the additional evidence needed rather than stretching one number to answer everything.

Answers and explanations
  1. frequency Turn 2. Frequency concerns how often interruptions occur; SAIFI falls from 1.2 to 1.0 on the supplied customer basis.
  2. denominator Turn 4. The denominator is the served-customer population used in the division, one thousand in both years of this comparison.
  3. system average Turn 6. A system average summarizes the population while allowing individual customers to have different counts, including none or several.
  4. SAIDI Turn 8. SAIDI is average total sustained-interruption duration per customer, calculated here as ninety and one hundred twenty minutes respectively.
  5. duration Turn 10. Duration here concerns accumulated interruption time; it rose even while the average interruption count fell.
  6. CAIDI Turn 12. CAIDI divides customer-minutes interrupted by customer interruptions, yielding seventy-five and one hundred twenty minutes on this consistent basis.
  7. event coverage Turn 14. Event coverage identifies which interruptions are included; a trend comparison requires consistent scope rather than mixing different event sets.
  8. reporting methodology Turn 16. Reporting methodology defines calculation and classification rules; changing those rules can undermine a comparison even when arithmetic is correct.
  9. target outcome Turn 18. A target outcome is an intended result, not a guaranteed effect; the supplied proposal lacks a validated impact estimate.
  10. resilience Turn 20. Resilience includes withstanding and recovering from disruption; a single annual average does not capture every aspect of that broader capability.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Celia: The headline says reliability improved on every measure, but the chart shows SAIDI increasing. I need a clear explanation before presenting this to community representatives.
  2. Ravi: Start with frequency. SAIFI is the average count of sustained interruptions per customer. It falls from one point two in Year 1 to one in Year 2.
  3. Celia: We get those figures by dividing twelve hundred customer interruptions and then one thousand by the same thousand customers served in each year.
  4. Ravi: Correct. Keep the denominator visible. It is the defined customer population, not only the customers who experienced interruptions, and it does not count network equipment failures.
  5. Celia: Residents will challenge an average of one if they personally had three interruptions. I want to explain that without dismissing their experience.
  6. Ravi: Exactly. A system average can coexist with uneven outcomes. Some customers may have none and others several; the average alone does not describe that distribution.
  7. Celia: Now the duration measure. We have ninety thousand customer-minutes in Year 1 and a hundred twenty thousand in Year 2, again across a thousand customers.
  8. Ravi: That gives SAIDI of ninety and one hundred twenty minutes. It measures total interruption time per customer over the year, not the length of one typical incident.
  9. Celia: Then interruptions became less frequent on average, but customers experienced more total interruption time on the system-average basis. That is a mixed trend, not universal improvement.
  10. Ravi: Yes. The duration measure worsened while frequency improved. We must say both, rather than choose whichever number supports the preferred headline about overall progress.
  11. Celia: Someone may ask how long an interruption lasted on average. That requires a different denominator from the number of customers served, does it not?
